JAPAN'S PARTNER
JAPANESE DEPENDENCE UPON THE UNITED STATES
BY ELIOT JANEWAY
(Reprinted by permission and through courtesy of Harper's Magazine)
The Japanese government is engaged in a war in which it
wouid be helpless without necessities-oil, steel, munitions, and
various commodities-which it imports from the United States.
At the same time the Roosevelt Administration has embarked
on the biggest naval program ever undertaken by our govern-
ment at any time, and one of the chief reasons for it is a fear of
the Japanese. How does it come about that our government
prepares to defend us against persons who would be no threat
if American industry did not help them to become one?
This year's great war games in the Pacific are more secret and
mysterious than for many years. This year's Congress is being
asked to vote a naval appropriations bill that will land America
right in the middle of the arms race. ...
. . It is Japan. not Germany or Italy, which figures as the
possible attacker in the popular mind and in the Navy Depart-
ment's plans. It is because of Japan that our enormous new
battleships will slide down the wavs in 1942 and 1913. There
it is, the smoke of the "China in ident," slowly creeping up trom
the horizon, a cloud by this time a little larger than a man's
hand, and our uneasy people find in it a warning that sometime.
from over the world's shoulder, the Japanese guns and battle-
ships may turn against us.
Meanwhile what is Japan doing? That is a fair question be-
cause it so happens that she is doing more than bombing Chinese
cities. Japan too has plans for 1943. Her immediate problem
is of course to supply the armies that are pushing into China:
but the program she has publicly announced, and upon which
she is now spending every last cent she can find, is not designed
merely to supply shells and tanks to her infantry or bomb、 to
her airmen. Japan is beginning a second industrial revolution.
the most immediate and obvious results of which cannot be
achieved before the carly 1940's. . ..
... Surely if Japan's new industries are to serve her... we
can only be delighted at the prospect of Japanese peasants
57

=== Page 140 === (confidence 0.99)
living an easier life. But this, alas, is not the case, and it seems
probable that the Japanese peasant will ride on the new rail-
roads only in troop trains, and will come to have a close ac-
quaintance with automobiles only if he is mobilized into a motor-
ized unit of infantry.
Japanese living standards are falling, not rising, as the blue-
prints for this program are approved. The textile industry-
Japan's greatest employer of labor and producer of wealth-is
seeing more and more of its plants close, as foreign exchange to
purchase cotton and wool abroad becomes rarer and rarer. Heav-
ier and heavier are the tax burdens falling upon the peasants.
and the Finance Minister can only express sympathy for their
decreasing incomes but can offer no hope of easier circumstances.
The Japanese government itself has sent a good part of the gold
reserve which nominally backs its currency out of the country to
pay for imports needed for the war.
Not only are these plans being formulated at an inopportune
moment, if these new industries are to serve any peacetime com-
mercial purposes, any solvent domestic market, but the develop
ment of them is to take place under the ægis of the government.
This may not seem remarkable, or out of place. Other govern-
ments than that of Japan are taking a hand in industrial plan-
ning. But in Japan the government means the Army. Only
one point need be cited for proof. The Japanese Army and Navy
between them account for seventy-nine per cent of the expendi-
tures under the 1938 budget, a budget that has quadrupled in
the seven years since the Manchurian conquest of i931. Japan's
plans for industrial expansion are plans for industrial armament.
This expansion is not confined to Japan proper. Second only
to the Mitsui combine in national power is the Manchurian
Heavy Industrial Development Co. Ltd., headed by Yoshisuke
Aikawa, which is the monopoly for expansion in Manchukuo-in
equal partnership with the government (i.e., the Army). The
Japanese Army owns large interests in many key industrial ven-
tures, notably the South Manchurian Railway, and is in effect
one of the Empire's most important property-owning bodies. . . .
.. . It is certain, . . . no matter how long Japan expects to
be formally fighting in China, that the northern provinces are
to be incorporated into Japan's long-term plans and to play their
part in strengthening her in future campaigns for future pros-
pective conquests. Here the Manchurian Heavy Industrial De
58

=== Page 141 === (confidence 0.98)
yelopment Co. has its twin in the Kochu Koshi, which is breed-
ing a swarm of "development companies" as subsidiaries. The
pattern is identical with that of Manchukuo. Here again is a
monopoly company operating in partnership with the govern-
ment, headed by a friend of the Army, and engaging in long-term
Army-inspired projects. . . . North China, still a battlefield. is
already, in the War Ministry's (areful blueprints, an interrelated
part of the cherished plan for Japanese self-sufficiency.
II
It will be worth while to examine a few of these industrial
schemes and so get some picture of the immense reorganization
and expansion which the Army hopes will make over Japan. By
1941 the steel industry is expected to be turning out twice as
much as at present. But not only is Japan's capacity for making
steel to be doubled: much of this new capacity is to be for special-
ized steel, so that Japan can thriftily turn out high-grade steel
for madhinery, and low-grade steel for office buildings. .. The
United States capacity, although enormously greater. is almost
altogether occupied by filling orders for industry. But Japan's
steel will be steel for the Army, whether it goes directly into
shells or less directly into madhinery to make light tanks. Japan
has no need of steel mills to turn out wire for farmers, or light
steel strips for refrigerators: tor she has no one to buy cither
wire or iceboxes.
Central agent in the steel program is a government corpora-
tion. the Japan Iron Manufacturing Co....
The automobile industry too is fa ing four years of rapid ex.
pansion under government regulation. The present session of
ihe Japanese Diet has indluded automobiles in the artiddes im-
portant from the standpoint of national "defense" which are
subjedt to complete govermment control. The purpose of in-
creased production is of course obvious-and is not for growing
sale to ivilians. . Japan's automobile factories, unable to cre-
ate demand in civilian cirdes. are working on special orders-for
the war. For some time now the Japanese automobile industry
has been concentrating on cars using Diesel motors-heavv. epen-
sive, cconomical only on very long hauls, and thus badly adapted
in every way for use by Japan's citizens, but extremely well
adapted to the needs of armies pushing into China's interior....

59

=== Page 144 === (confidence 0.99)
The other method of transport at the Army's disposal, the rail-
roads, is making almost as much trouble. The government au-
thorities used to be of the comfortable opinion that they had
plenty of cars and locomotives to handle both peace- and war-
time shipments. But they are now finding 1o0,ooo tons of freight
going unhandled every day for lack of transport facilities, and
new railroad equipment is vital. To complicate the problem are
the 1,2oo miles of railroad taken over in North China which-
perhaps simply to confuse the future conqueror-were built on
three gauges, wide, standard, and narrow. So orders are being
placed under Army supervision-for 500 locomotives, 1,o00 pas-
senger cars, and 1o,ooo freight cars. The steel is being obtained
from our old friend, the Japan Iron Manufacturing Co.
. . . Military expansion for steel, automobiles, and railroad
equipment depends on military expansion of the machine-tool
industry which fashions steel into parts for these basic steel-using
industries. We have the government's guarantee that the ma-
chine-tool business can depend on at least two years of military
expansion after any need for it in China is over. Is any other
evidence needed to show that North China is just another step
in what Japan regards as her imperial progress?
Oil is notoriously the weakest link in Japan's economy. Obvi-
ously, as this program of industrial expansion goes into effect her
fuel requirements will increase. Now unless something is done
to permit Japan to lay her hands on oil that is exclusively hers,
this whole complicated program will be jeopardized. But Japan's
oil deposits are negligible, as are those of Manchuria and North
China. Japan, however, is not going to drill for oil, but to man-
ufacture it. She is planning to spend the better part of a billion
yen on a project for liquefying coal. . ..
Two commercial commodities have been included in this self-
sufficiency program for military reasons. Japan's cotton goods
industry needs, when working at top speed, about 412 million
bales of cotton a year. China produces about 33/4 million bales,
most of it in the North. Japan plans to increase this output by
persuading the peasants to give up growing food crops and con-
fine themselves to cotton culture, depending on food imported
from other regions. Pulp output, used by her rayon industry,
is also to be increased.
For Japan is anxious to maintain her position as a textile ex-
porter, even though at present she is starving out many of her
60

=== Page 145 === (confidence 1.00)
small textile manufacturers. Textile exports bring her foreign
exchange which can be used for the import of goods which her
ambitious program demands.
III
Now this is important to notice because it is the first admis-
sion that this enormous program, so calmly discussed in Japan,
is not self-inaugurating and self-perpetuating. It is dependent
on imports. So vital are the imports that the government has
had to include in its plans two industries, only minutely con-
cerned with the Army, because they make these imports possible.
Steel, automobiles, railroad equipment, machine tools, oil, the
plans are smugly and snugly laid for expansion in these fields on
a huge scale. But here is the catch. Not only a prostrate China
is to contribute to the program. So is Somebody Else, the nation
which sells industrial equipment to Japan.
If you are beginning to have a grim suspicion that Somebody
Else is the United States-which is boycotting Japanese goods.
fearing Japanese aggression, building battleships to defend her-
self against Japan-you are quite right. You will have heard of
our scrap iron shipments to Japan. We are still selling her
scrap. More important, we are selling her steel, finished and
semi-finished, ready to be processed by her factories into arma-
ments. We are selling her gasoline to fill the tanks of the air-
planes that bomb Chinese cities. And most important of all,
from our own standpoint, we are selling her the machine tools
and metal-working machinery which she is to use in revolution-
izing her economy on to a base more nearly comparable with our
own. In the 189o's there was a bit of excitement in England over
the fact that England was exporting textile machinery to coun-
tries, including Japan, though no one thought much about her
then, which were substantial markets for English textiles. Little
attention was paid to the outery. and the trade continued. What
Japanese textiles subsequently did to English markets is a matter
of economic history. Well, that took over forty years and there
was never any question of war between England and Japan. nor
were the machinery exports for armament use. This. then, is a
very minor analogy to what we are doing at present. The great
triumph which has made the United States the most powerful
industrial nation in the world has been the development of mass
production-for pea eful purposes. We are selling Japan the
imeans of mass production for war.
61

=== Page 146 === (confidence 0.99)
But before we begin to talk about the machinery we are sell-
ing Japan for future use it is worth while to put down some-
thing of what we are selling Japan to-day for immediate use.
For this forms, indirectly, part of her expansion program too.
Her program, as we have seen, is based upon the complete con-
trol, for her own purposes, of North China. We are helping her
to conquer North China by selling her the vital raw materials
she needs for armaments.
Steel first of all. In 1937 Japan produced 5.3 million tons of
steel. Well over one-third of that steel was made from American
raw materials, scrap and pig iron. Besides raw material for her
furnaces, the United States sold her 1,ooo,ooo tons of new iron
and steel in various unfabricated forms. This was quite an in-
crease from the 1936 figure of 54.00o tons. And Japan bought
75,ooo tons of American pig iron alone during the first two
months of 1938, and is ordering 100,o00 tons more. The same
huge rate of increase is continuing in the purchase of steel. Our
exports to Japan and Manchukuo in January 1938 were three
times what they had been a year ago. A very important point
to note in connection with Japan's purchases of steel here is that
much of it is specialty steel already alloyed with expensive special
metals-copper, nickel, aluminum, chromium, and so on-which
Japan is thus spared from buying. and, indeed, which she is still
unable to process adequately for modern engineering and arma-
ment use. 'Even so, she bought more than twice as much refined
copper from us in 1937 as she did in 1936.
Japan's oil self-sufficiency program, you will recall, will not be
completed until 1943, and even then will give her at best but
two-thirds of her gasoline requirements, and 45 per cent of her
heavy oil needs. During 1937, when this program was merely
so many figures on paper, Japan bought 31,ooo,ooo barrels of oil
abroad. By October she had already bought 23,ooo,ooo barrels
of it from us. This includes special aviation fuel, produced in
commercial quantities only by America. This anti-knock gaso-
line, high in octane content, is the only fuel which will permit
some of the American engines which Japan has been buying to
develop their full power. Because of its anti-knock qualities too.
it lengthens the life of engines, thus saving Japan's under-capaci-
tied engine shops much replacement work. So essential is oil to
Japan, and so precarious her present shortage, that Tokyo taxis
are being prevented from cruising in order to conserve her gaso-
line supplies.
62

=== Page 147 === (confidence 0.99)
But even more important than the crucial oil and steel sup-
plies for immediate use that we are sending Japan is our part
in aiding Japan to complete her expansion program. Her ex-
pansion program for the steel industry calls for an increase of
2.3 million tons of rolled steel (finished steel rolled from steel
ingots). In 1937 Japan contra ted for the purchase of three mod-
ern American rolling mills having a combined capacity of some
million and a quarter tons-almost half of the increase called for
by Japan's five-year program. These are the famous new auto-
matic continuous sheet-and-strip mills made only in this coun-
try, which eliminate So-85 per cent of the labor usually needed
for rolling steel and which complete the complicated process at
the rate of about i.Soo feet a minute, ten times as fast as the old
fashioned mills through which steel was rolled by hand. The
steel thus turned out lends itself particularly well to automobile
production (another vital part of Japan's expansion program)
and, by their labor saving devices. these mills will ease somewhat
the serious Japanese shortage of skilled workmen.
It is not only steel rolling mills which we are selling Japan.
but high-speed metal-working madhinery of all kinds. We sold
Japan S3p,ooo,ooo worth in 1937. more than she had bought
from the whole world in 1936. and about two-thirds of her 1937
imports. But machinery imports are much more important than
their dollar value would indicate: cadh machine in its lifetime
produces goods worth many times the value of its purchase price.
Moreover. the high-speed production and standardization of op
crations which efficient machinery makes possible is something
that cannot be measured in dollars or tons. It is the very basis
of mass production and the enormous efficiency that that implies
-for peace or for war. Japan's automobile industry, for instance.
is faced at present with a confusing variety of machinery and
parts: standardization is admittedly its principal problem. Aik
awa stated, as we have seen. that two years would be necessars
for the installation of modern, high-speed standardized equip
ment in his Mandhurian automobile and aircraft plants. It is
because he must have standardized and efficient machinery, and
because such madhinery for automobile production is made only
in the United States, that he is coming here shortly to spend
Szoo,ooo,ooo-almost a third of the total cost of the Manchurian
development program-on American equipment-if his sales talk
about profits to be made in the development of Manchukuo per
suades American industrialists to advance the $3oo,ooo,ooo Mr.
63

=== Page 148 === (confidence 0.99)
Aikawa needs. And if Japan begins to standardize her auto, her
steel, her engineering industries on the basis of American designs,
she cannot later turn to those of another country; if she did she
would simply continue the same old confusion caused by diverse
types of machinery, some of them interlocking, others not, which
she is trying to get rid of now. Parallel to her auto-equipment
buying, purchases of American locomotives and freight cars will
facilitate the completion of Japan's continental railway program.
Twenty per cent of the new rolling stock Japan needs will be
bought here.
Japan's textile exports have traditionally been the most im-
portant way in which she has financed whatever purchases of
capital goods she has had to make. But textile exports depend
upon raw material imports, notably of cotton. Last year, when
Japan found that the China incident was developing into some-
thing very much like a full-fledged war, she was forced to curtail
these imports, sacrificing her cotton textile industry and its ex-
ports (its export revenues as well, it may be added) to her need
for materials of war. The immediate drop in export revenues.
and thus in foreign exchange, forced her to resort to large-scale
shipments of gold which cut deeply into her scanty reserves. Al-
together she shipped $25o,ooo,o0o in gold to the United States
in 1937; and some $25,o00,o00 more has arrived in California so
far this year, with more promised. Furthermore, she is now de-
bating whether or not to ship every last remaining ounce of gold
to us, since her need of manufactured imports is much more
vital to her than the farce of a gold foundation  her paper yen.
These gold payments went for armainent necessities and for
machinery needed by the expansion program. They did not go
for cotton. Thus was set in motion the vicious circle which has
closed her textile factories, despite her recognition of the im-
portance of export revenues to her present solvency and future
plans. By heroic measures, she succeeded since the current cot-
ton season began in August in cutting down her lint takings here
to a third of last year's volume. In spite of this, however, she
ended 1937 with the enormous trade deficit of 636,ooo,o00 yen.
This appalling figure frightened her into cutting down her cot-
ton imports by an additional 18o,ooo,ooo yen in January and
February, so that her trade deficit for these months was reduced
by 75 per cent.
But the unfortunate result was that she found herself without
sufficient cotton goods on hand to fill export orders previously
64

=== Page 149 === (confidence 0.99)
undertaken. To fill these orders, she has had to purchase fin-
ished cotton goods here, an unprecedented step; and, however
reluctantly, to resume her raw cotton purchases here. And as
soon as she resumed her cotton purchases she was compelled
to resume her gold shipments as well, to the dismay of what is
left of conservative opinion in Japan. But sad as the result has
been for Japan, it has been sadder for us. Cotton is the Achilles
heel of our economy. Japan's inability to purchase her normal
quota of our cotton has had a most depressing effect on the
cotton market and, therefore, on Southern purchasing power.
And her orders to our steel and machine-tool industries have
been far from making up what her boycott of our cotton has cost
us. Similarly, Japan's inability to supply her rayon industry
with pulp has hurt our West Coast lumiber mills, which suffered
a panic last summer when the outbreak of war left them with
huge unexportable surpluses on hand. The Japanese purchases
that have been outlined here, vital as they are to Japan. were
sufficient in 1937 to provide our steel and oil industries with a
week's work apiece. no more. They are essential to Japan but
unimportant to us, and the long-term program of armament
expansion growing out of the war in China which they are making
possible has ominous political implications for the United States.
Well, there is the story, a very simple one. Japan is the one
nation in the world whose aggression we fear. Japan is the un-
ashamed attadker of a country with whom we svmpathize, whose
magnificent defensive stand we admire heartily. In this attack
have occurred atrocities-the bombing of universities, for exam-
ple-which shock the world.
American industry is selling Japan the goods which permit her
to do this and to rear grandiose schemes for continuing to do
this on a scale so huge that all western Asia will be reduced to the
level of the Japanese subjects in Korea. Seventy-five per cent
of the gasoline Japan used last vear. gasoline for tanks and
bombers and warships. came from the United States. One third
of the steel she made last vear. steel for shells, bombs, dum dum
bullets, was made out of American raw materials.
Here, then, is the paradox. The Japanese menace is made
possible by American exports. Because of the Japanese menace
our government is building battleships with money which might
otherwise go for housing or to case thie tax burden of our people.
What is to be done about this situation is up to the American
people. Here are the facts.

JAPAN'S NEW NEED: AMERICAN STEELS,
MACHINES AND OILS
BY ELIOT JANEWAY
(Reprinted through courtesy and permission of Asia Magazine)
It must have been a good five years ago that some one discov-
ered Japanese purchases of American scrap iron. Ever since that
time-and particularly since the beginning of the Japanese cam-
paign in China last year-the United States has been collectively
shaken, every now and then, by a spasm of conscience over the
scrap iron which is mysteriously being transmuted, under Nip-
pon's cherry blossoms, into bombs to kill Chinese babies. Car-
toons decrying this trade, and the trade in oil to turn the wheels
of Japanese automobiles, the propellers of Japanese airplanes.
and to push her battleships through the Yellow Sea, have become
familiar. But the typical American reaction has been the thought
that, after all, Japan would make such purchases from Germany
or England if she did not make them from the United States:
and the trade has gone on.
I want to talk now about a trade with Japan which could be
carried on by the United States alone, which no one has car-
tooned, and which is qualitatively of such vast importance that,
without it, the probability is Japan's economy would break
down.
This is the trade in machinery and in lubricating oils. Japan
is buying from the United States machinery which her German
ally and her British ex-ally do not make at all, or do not make
in a quantity or quality able to challenge American products.
It is true that Japan could buy elsewhere some-but not so much
-of the scrap iron and crude oil which bulk so large in her im-
ports. But, now that she has begun to deal in and to depend on
American machinery, American machine tools, special American
steels, lubricating oils and so on, she cannot turn away-unless
she starts to build up her heavy industries all over again, and her
engineering processes from the bottom, and on a less efficient
basis. For Japan has begun to modernize and integrate her
industry with the aid of the United States. One of the most
important parts of integration on a mass production scale is, ob-
viously, standardization. Because she is using American models
now, she must continue to use American models. And in addi-
66

=== Page 151 === (confidence 0.99)
tion, and in explanation of her natural impulse to turn first to
the United States, is the fact that German and British industry.
old and expert as they are in engineering skills, do not use those
skills in the efficient mass production way which is so typically
American, nor are they at present able to employ those skills on
engineering goods for the export market. It is in any case absurd
to think of Japan's dividing her coonomy into two or three com-
partiments, modeled by engineering imports from different na-
tions. Since her main line of mass production development in
the heavy industries is American, she must either complete her
program under American auspices or at great expense scrap the
better part of her American engineering models and start all over
again on a German or British basis.
Japan's in reasing imports of American machinery show how
thoroughly her quantitatively larger imports of raw materials
are, and will be, influenced and controlled by the machinery
which must process them. Thus a qualitative problem has arisen
beside her older problem of obtaining raw materials. They must
be raw materials of a suitable quality and kind.
Now just what is the importance of machinery to a country?
Let us take a very simple case. Japan is capable of turning out
5.ooo,ooo tons of steel a vear. when she is buving scrap and pig
iron abroad (that is, in the United States) freely. That sound
impressive, but actually it is jtst a first step. For steel is just so
much raw material for modern industry, able to do nothing but
rust in a mill vard unless it is handled further. If it is to be
useful to modern industries. it must be turned, ground. cut.
shaped, welded, treated to resist heat and acid, to cut other steel.
to endure enormous pressures and great speeds. In the past five
years, machine tools for making madhinery out of steel have been
speeded up as mudh as has been the making of the steel itself.
Éver more efficient tools have been developed to handle the
growing output of the steel mills. Thus. if Japan is to use the
steel, she must import the tools, or she will find herself produe
ing steel she cannot process. And to produce and process steel
at this pace she is calling upon the engineers and chemists and
plants of Detroit and Pitsburgh. So American machinery. in
effect, rules the greatest of Japan's empire-building industries.
These empire-building and controlling industries have changed
with the empire itself. Japan first of all built up her strength as
an island empire. The industries she developed were, naturally.
shipping and shipbuilding. and light goods for export, such as
67

=== Page 152 === (confidence 1.00)
textiles, just like that other island empire halfway across the
world; which has prided herself on her textile exports and her
sea-going tradition. But now Japan is becoming, and desiring
ever more to become, a mainland empire. Her problems are
changing. Control of her tens of, thousands of square miles of
backward territory, peopled by unwilling subjects, raises new
questions of continental transportation and communication. It
is the United States and not England which has developed the
technique of handling industries such as railroad, automobile and
aircraft building on a mass production scale, and it is therefore
to the United States that Japan has turned for aid in the solution
of her new problems; and particularly for the machinery to set
American-style assembly lines in motion. It is very evident that
an assembly line industry, importing its machinery or at the best
the machine tools that make its machinery, is qualitatively de-
pendent for its very life on the engineering skill ten thousand
miles away that determined the position of each screw in a cer-
tain machine and decided that it should operate at four hundred
and fifty revolutions per minute.
Because of this long-distance dependence. Japan needs machin-
ery that is well built and sturdy, since she herself is not equipped
to duplicate or repair it. In any case, she must mortgage her
industrial future to the nation that supplies her, since replace-
ment must be made frequently. Auto-making machinery, for
instance, is subjected to great pressure and must be replaced
every three to five years. And there is always the chance of an
accident which will hold up an assembly line until repairs-which
Japan is ill equipped to take care of-can be made.
Thus far we have been going on the assumption that these
complicated machines are imported to handle the 5,ooo,ooo tons
of steel which Japan produces yearly. But this is hardly true.
For the steel which Japan turns out is, for the most part, heavy
and brittle, unsuited for use in high speed and high tension
machines and motors. So Japan imports not only American
tools but also new American steels alloyed with aluminum,
nickel, chrome and other metals, and much lighter and more
durable than the typical Japanese product. Railroad equipment
is one of the chief products which Japan must turn out. It is
obvious that light steel cars, which are yet strong enough to
carry a heavy load, are advantageous.
In the aircraft industry this special steel is not simply advan-
tageous; it is indispensable. The American Machinist, a trade
68

=== Page 153 === (confidence 0.99)
paper, mentions casually the composition of steel used in an
airplane propeller made by a famous American manufacturer.
The propeller is forged from a special alloy of aluminum and
steel which has been heat-treated until its tensile strength has
become sufficient to enable it to withstand a strain of 55,o00
pounds per square inch. It must be able to stand such a strain
if it is to serve the powerful engine that drives it. Japan is buy-
ing such engines in the United States, and she is purchasing also
the machine tools designed to make these propellers. But even
when she has installed them she will still have to come to the
United States for the aluminum steels out of which the propellers
are made.
For in Japan steel is still just steel. while in the United States
steel is bought according to speification. The consumer tells
the manufacturer what he wants to use his steel for, and the man-
ufacturer melts it accordingly. Japan is finding it as necessary
as does the United States to use special. efficient steels for special
purposes. She is. for instance. and very immediately. planning
to motorize her infantry. She wants thousands of fast, sturdy
autos, trucks and tanks, with which to send her troops into
China's vast interior. There are no service stations in Shansi.
and, if each part of the cars Japan gives to her soldiers is not
strong and reliable, the Japanese advance will end only in a
bogged down mess of broken cars surrounded by guerrilla snip-
ers. Now, if vou want to cut teeth in automobile gears at a mass
production clip, the cutting tool sou use will have to be fash-
ioned precisely to cut five one thousandths of an inch deep. and
at the rate of 425 feet a minute. But no ordinary steel can be
handled in this fashion and survive to function as a gear on an
automobile. Accordingly. Japan is making her gears with Amer-
ican machine tools. The steel and the tools are useless if
they are not used together, the steel because an old-fashioned
tool cannot cut it, the tools because they will destrov a less
strongly fashioned steel. And still Japan has not remotely begun
to approach the American standard of efficiency which grades all
steels meticulously according to the uses their compositions per-
mit them to be put to.
Much of Japan's 5.ooo,ooo-ton steel capacity must, therefore.
be written off as useless in the face of her railroad, automobile
and aircraft program. If Japan hopes seriously ever to turn her
feudal peasant land into a power comparable to America's indus-
trialized might, she must start from the very beginning to dupli-
6g

=== Page 154 === (confidence 0.99)
cate America's industrial plants. She has, during the past year,
begun to purchase modern mills for rolling steel in the most
efficient way. And, because of this, she has been forced to in-
crease her purchases of machinery to process this new steel. Dur-
ing 1937 she spent as much on machinery as she spent on her
much heralded purchases of scrap iron, and she has bought so
far this year at the record rate of S3.ooo,ooo a month.
But the purchases of machinery in which her purchases of
steel involve her have forced Japan also to pay tribute to another
industry, unimportant in the extreme as far as quantity is con-
cerned, but qualitatively, again, absolutely necessary once mod-
ern industrial methods begin to be followed. This is the
American method of lubrication-developed to its height in the
machine shops of Cleveland and Youngstown. All of the com-
plicated and expensive machinery Japan imports is fitted with
oil pumps, designed to force just the right grade and kind of
lubricating oil between metal surfaces at just the right speed.
and thus, by preventing friction, to lengthen the life of the tool
or the engine. And so Japan must acknowledge the hold over
her economy of yet another unique American product. which
hardly shows up as a significant item in her list of imported
goods and yet without which an integrated modern economy
would be out of the question. The steel takes the tools, and the
tools take the oils, and Japan must come to the United States for
all three.
Every day Japan's war in China makes her need of this Amer-
ican equipment more urgent. The railway problem is typical.
Because of a car shortage, 1oo,ooo tons of freight remain un-
handled every day. Japan plans to build zoo locomotives and
7,o00 freight cars. But her 24 plants dedicated to this purpose
must remain idle until the lathes and other machine tools she
has ordered abroad, principally in America, arrive. Meanwhile
she has placed emergency orders for 2o per cent of this program
in America, abandoning hope of supplying them herself.. ..
And so the story, all through the industrial economy that
Japan is striving so desperately to modernize and to integrate, is
of dependence on American products, on unique American skills,
on goods which America alone is producing in quantities suffi-
cient to export. For light, durable steels in mass production
quantities; for railroad, automobile and aircraft manufacturing
equipment made from these steels and by these tools; and, finally.
70

=== Page 155 === (confidence 0.98)
for the high-powered fuel needed to run her new American air-
craft engines, Japan can go nowhere else. And each of these
products is uscless without all the others.
What Japan is aiming at-and indeed what these American
products are alone designed to serve-is an interdependent, mass
production economy. But what Japan is necessarily and inevita-
bly involving herself in is an economy integrated toward contin
ued imports from one country, interdependent indeed, but far
from independent of that country's good will. And that economy
is virtually subject to the control, no matter how little her pub-
licists and generals may know it or care to admit it, of the quan-
titatively small, but qualitatively all important engineering goods
which that country sends her.

The difficulties the Japanese have been encountering in their
war against China spotlights what singularly few people seem
aware of: That the United States is in the strategic position of
being able to cripple Japan's war mathine by suddenly cutting
off the supply of scrap needed in steel production. This may be
illustrated by citing figures for Japan. In 1937 she produced

roughly 5,goo,ooo tons of steel. In the manufacture of this ton-

since Japan invaded China, of the vital role played in Japan's
war economy by American industry.
AMERICAN EXPORTS BOLSTER JAPAN
BY T. A. BISsON
(Reprinted through courtesy and permission of Foreign
Policy Association, Inc.)
During recent weeks the new military-fascist members added
to Japan's Cabinet have demonstrated how the war in China
should be conducted. Not content with an intensified drive on
the central China fronts, they have instituted a ruthless bombing
of Canton which has piled up a total of at least 8,ooo casualties,
mostly civilian non-combatants. Laying claim to "military neces-
sity," Japanese officials have spurned protests against this bar-
barity, declared that the air raids would be continued "with even
greater vigor," and acted on this declaration. By June 12, as a
result, Canton was experiencing its fifteenth successive day of
aerial bombardment.
Military inefficiency rather than military necessity-if the real-
ization that a desperate case requires desperate remedies be ex-
cepted-lies at the root of this latest exhibition of Japancse
"frightfulness." For months Japan's naval planes have been
bombing the tracks, stations and bridges of the Canton-Kowloon
Railway in an effort to disrupt the munitions shipments which
are reaching Hankow via Hongkong and Canton. Japancse
78

=== Page 157 === (confidence 0.99)
planes have been virtually unopposed in the air; nevertheless.
they have failed to interrupt railway traffic. Heroic Chinese re-
pair crews quickly restored tracks or bridges where the raiding
planes scored direct hits. Hoping to demoralize the Chinese
defense, the new Japanese leadership deliberately resorted to an
indiscriminate bombing of Canton. The doubtful efficacy of
this weapon has again been illustrated in south China: latest
reports indicate that the Canton-Kowloon Railway is still func-
tioning and that munitions trains are still passing north to Han-
kow.
The raids on Canton are not the responsibility of Japan alone.
Americans should face the fact that this country is participating
de facto in the destruction wrought at Canton. The high test
aviation fuel, which the United States is supplying in large and
increasing quantities to Japan, makes this country a participant
in the Canton bombardments: so. also, does the S5.67o.237 worth
of American aircraft and accessories sold to Japan since January
1938. As pointed out in the Washington News Letter, these facs
led Secretary Hull on June 1i to reveal that the State Depare
ment was taking measures to "dis ourage" sale of American air-
planes to Japan. Airplanes, however, constitute but a small
portion of the war materials which American concerns are sup
plying to Japan. High-grade steels with specified allovs. special
types of machinery and madhine tools, and certain fuel and
lubricating oils-these, and other products, are available to Japan
in large quantities only in the American market. When scrap
and pig iron, refined copper. petroleum, automobiles and accts
sories, non-metallic metals, and numerous lesser items are added
to the list of American exports to Japan, the full picture bec omes
apparent. A scientific study of this subject, to be shortly pub-
lished by the Chinese Council for Economic Research at Wash-
ington, D. C., estimates that during recent months Japan has
secured 544 per cen: of its war imports from the United States
It is clear that Secretary Hull's private warnings to a few
American airplane manufa turers do not constitute an adequate
answer to the question raised by these facts. The U'nited Siates.
as the chief source of supply of Japan's war materials, has become
a silent partner in Japanese aggression. It is not enough to sav
that this country is also supplying munitions to China. Nor does
application of the Neutrality Act commend itself as the proper
answer to the situation that has developed. As early a、 last
September the American government explicitly ranged itself
73

=== Page 158 === (confidence 0.99)
with the League in condemnation of Japan's bombing of open
cities in China; in more recent declarations, the State Depart-
ment has implicitly branded Japan as the aggressor in the cur-
rent Far Eastern hostilities. Something more than words is
required, however, if the United States is not to be accused of
transparent hypocrisy. In order to enforce a policy of non-par-
ticipation in Japanese aggression, the American government
must levy a strict embargo on sales of war materials to Japan.
At the same time, the Chinese government should continue to
enjoy every facility for the purchase of the munitions which it
needs to protect China's national existence against an unjustified
and ruthless assault. Only thus can the deeds of our governiment
be squared with its words. This policy will demand a price
from certain vested American interests, but that price will be
cheap compared with the war which looms on the horizon if
Japan ever establishes itself effectively in China.
Despite Japan's recent military gains, there is no reason to
believe that the Chinese cause is hopeless. Rising waters and
breaches in the dikes of the Yellow River have mired the Jap-
anese advance along the Lunghai Railway. and Cheng how is
apparently still in Chinese hands. Time is being given for the
Chinese forces to reform their lines for the defense of Hankow.
Withdrawal of further American aid to the aggressor at this (ru
cial moment would condemn Japan's desperate eleventh-hour
offensive to defeat.
Japan furnishes the pilot-Ameria furnishes the airplane, gaso-
line, oil and bombs for the ravaging of undefended Chinese cities.
74

=== Page 159 === (confidence 0.98)
Stop Economic Aid to Japan ...
WHAT ORGANIZATIONS CAN DO
The active support of organizations throughout America is
needed in order to make effective an embargo upon war supplies
from the United States to Japan.
Any or all of the following suggested activities can be carried
out. beginning at once. by organizations, national or local, with
or without the cooperation of other bodies:
1. Take the stoppage of military supplies to Japan as a leading
issue in connedtion with organizational publications, correspond
ence, meetings and conferenees. Arrange for special gatherings
and radio addresses when possible.
2. Send copies of all actions taken by large or small groups to
President Roosevelt, to Seoetany of State Cordell Hull. to mem-
bers of the Senate and House Committees on Foreign Relations.
to Senators and Congressmen of sour state, and to local news
papers.
3. Write individual lettes to an or all of the above protes
ing against American aid to Japanese aggression and requesting
that ofh ial a tion be taken to piohibit the export of war supplits
to Japan.

of women's oiganizations, labor organizations, and peace groups
and other publie spirited itizens to call on Congressmen. Sen

opinion of local groups, and urging them to take a position
with regard to the issue.
5. It the legislatune in von state is meeting this autumn or

supplies and financial aid trom Japan for the duration of the war.
or withholding cconomic aid for war purposes to countries whch
are in process of violating peatce neaties with the United States
6. Express vour moral support to industrialists, menchants

for Japan's war madhine.

WHAT INDIVIDUALS CAN DO
The actions of individuals are no less significant than those
of organizations. To any who feel strongly with regard to the
question of American support for Japanese aggression, the fol-
lowing activities are suggested:
1. Send letters to President Roosevelt, Secretary of State Cor-
dell Hull and to Senators and Congressmen from your state, as
suggested in third paragraph above.
2. Write the editor of your local newspaper, stating your po-
sition and suggesting that the paper lend its editorial support
to the policy of non-participation in Japanese aggression.
3. Order and distribute further copies of the pamphlet,
"America's Share in Japan's War Guilt."
4. Discuss the question with friends and think of further
means toward ending America's support to Japan at this time.
Send your ideas and suggestions to the Secretary, American Com-
mittee for Non-Participation in Japanese Aggression.
5. Promote and cooperate in such organizational activities as
are suggested above.

=== Page 161 === (confidence 0.99)
American Committee for Non-Participation
in Japanese Aggression ...
A growing realization that the United States is. in effect, an
ally of Japan for her program of conquest in Asia has led to the
spontaneous organization of the American Committce for Non-
Participation in Japanese Aggression.
Neither widespread sympathy for the Chinese people in their
courageous struggle for freedom nor protests over inhuman war-
fare and the violation of treaties can atone for the positive
support which we are lending to the Japanese military niachine.
This Committee's reason for existence is to help in bringing
the essential facts to the attention of the American people, co-
operating with other individuals and organizations that are
working to this end, and to investigate ways and means whereby
the stoppage of war credits and essential war supplies to Japan
can be made effective for the remainder of the present conflidt
in China.
Significant facts with regard to American aid to Japan will
be placed before commercial, industrial. financial, "labor and
transportation groups. On grounds of both moral responsibility
and far-sighted self-interest, the case for non-participation in
Japanese aggression will be presented, and public opinion will
be invited to lend its support to all who voluntarily sacrifice a
share in "profits from the blood and tears of the innocent" in
China.
The Committee will endeavor to help in crystallizing public
demand and support for governmental executive action designed
to check the flow of American credits and war materials to Japan.
Study is being made of the forms of effective executive action
possible under existing law. Contact will be maintained with the
governmental departments concerned.
The Committee will also work toward securing the enactment
of a measure at the next session of Congress prohibiting the (
tension of economic aid for war purposes to a country which is
in process of violating a solemn treaty of peace to which the
United States is a party.
To insure the more complete effectiveness of any action taken
in America, the Committee will collaborate with organizations
77

=== Page 162 === (confidence 1.00)
in other countries which are working to halt shipments of essen-
tial war materials to Japan. Through this means, international
action by peoples and governments will be sought.
The central and single aim of the Committee is expressed in
the phrase "non-participation in Japanese aggression." Doc-
trinal isms, domestic issues, and the European situation, how-
ever important, are beyond its scope.
Members of the Committee regard this program as a construc-
tive effort in the direction of international peace and justice.
They desire the well-being of the people of Japan as well as of
all other peoples in the Pacific area. They desire peace and ju
tice for all concerned. They feel, at the same timie, that before
any just settlement and lasting peace structure can be achieved.
Japanese military aggression must be checked. They believe that
for this a policy of non-cooperation by the powers together with
continued Chinese resistance will be effective, and that the urgent
and crucial need of the moment is to withhold economic assist-
ance for war purposes from Japan.
Starting in a small way, this Committee has received an imme-
diate response on all sides. This has led to a more inclusive organ-
ization which is still in process as the movement to check the flow
of war naterials from America to Japan gains rapidly in momen-
tum. The contents of this booklet suggest the contacts which
the Committee has made to date and the extent to which the
movement has already grown. It is expected that formal organi-
zation of the Committee will be completed and announced in
the near future, with outstanding citizens among the sponsors.
Far more important than this Committee, however, is the
movement of which it is but a part. Not one but scores of or-
ganizations and thousands of individuals must unite in common
effort in order to make this movement effective.
In cooperation with individuals and organizations throughout
the United States, furnishing and receiving suggestions and in-
formation, the American Committee for Non-Participation in
Japanese Aggression is ready to do its part.
78

TEXT OF A TELEGRAPHIC ESSAGE RECEI VED AT THE
CHINESE E BASSY, DATED CIUNGKING, AUGUST 27, 1938
Ten bodies of the victims of the Chinese National Aviation
Corporation passenger plane tragedy were recovered up to yester-
day evening. Almost all bear machine gun bullet wounds. Eight
bodies were identified includin the two prominent Shanghai
bankers Mr. Hsu Sineloh and r. Hu Pikieng. The bullet riddled
wreckage of the plane was also salvaged.
The Chinese force withdrew from Juichang, Kiangsi, on
the 25th taking up positions in the west and northwest of Juichm g
beyond the range of the Japanese naval guns.
On the western shore of the Poyang Lake the Chinese wiped
out three hundred Japanese landed at Niuweitun, south of Singtse,
on the 24th. The last remnants were exterminated following a
three hour battle yesterday morning.
On the northern side of the Yangtse River, the Japanese in
Hofei, Anhwei, started a westward offensive against Liuan. Serious
fighting is taking place in a point 30 kilometers east of Liuan.

Dear Mr. Secretar:

Department of State
I can harily expect
 remomber me as you have so ma
894.2%
ers coming to you continuously from every where. I am the man from
China whom, with his son, you v ry graciously received on July ?2nd.
That son will never forget t at exnerience and your w rds lirect to
him. We are both very rateful to you.
As you may see from the above I am now on my way back to China.
I do not need to tell you that I go with a heavy heart. Yes, I am
thinking of the suffering Ch nese. I am thinking of the War-Mad
militarists , of the inv ders from Japan and their continued an con-
tinuing brutalities. But these are not what hurts me most: I am
thinking, I am hurt, stunned, ashamed that our country continues to
8
be a partner, an ally by furnish ng the invader the materials, at
least a large persent of tlem, to keep tjos terrible holocaust going.
Surely you and theepeople at lerge do not fully realize what is
happening, what has been going on now for over a year. Get the pic-
ture! Just imagine that an enemy ha treated your fair c ty, our
capital, as Nanking has been treated an t at it ha been ma e poss-
3
ible by the help of En land or some other nati n caliming to be our
3
friend. (Do we not continue to caaim to be a friend of China?) What
3
would we thin of such frien ship? Look again! Your home has been
entered by a neighbor across th sr ot; your sons have been bayonet-
ed to death; your wife has b en assaulted by a ozen or more of the
brutes; your daughters have been raped several times and then forced
to go with these beasts to satisfy their lusts, to bear children fdr
them! Your home has been looted of all that was of any value. Yet,
all the while your neighbors stood by and looked on and did nothing.
Alas! That would havebben bad enough but that is not all the story
by any means. Some of those NlGilsuRS furnished the bayonets, the
guns, even provided the cars fill d with gasoline for that neighbor
across the street" to spirit your aughters way to that life which
is worse than death. What kind of fifendship is that? As I see it,
that is our position as we wontinue to share in this tragedy.
Since we are furnishing the invader with 54.4% of her materiaP
FILED
it seems to me that we must share that percent of her cruelties, (
atrocities, rapes and murderw. "How long, Oh Lord, How Long?"
I have read with interest your recent utterance. I know it
does not matter that I indorse them but I wish to say that I do. I
hope you will believe me when I say that on my way to the U.S. A.
in Maroh, before I had heard your name mentioned in that relationship, 
I said to myself and my fellow travellers, "Our Secretary of State
should be the next President." After meeting you in your office I
am more than ever convinced that the American people could not make
a better choice. I am saying this not because I am a Tennessean.
nor is it mere empty words, but because I believe it to be the truth.
I sincerely hope it may be realized.

=== Page 188 === (confidence 0.99)
2
ut what I am writing about today cannot waat till then. Multi-
des perish daily. Civilization is threatened. Can't something be
done now? Mark you, I do not want war. Far from it. War settles
nothing!'I believe there is another way out. I do not n ed to tell
you that Japan is boycotting our goods, that is, she is buying only
what she wants to buy. Why cannot a nation use the same liberty in
selling what she wants to sell? veryone knows that Japan has broken
faith. Her signature to treaties is as nothing to her if she wishes
to do otherwise. Why are we compelled to do business with her since
she has put herself in this position? I do not think I am telling
you anything new when I say that Japan has no more respect for our
flag, for our wishes that she does for any sort of rag in the hands
of a coolie in China. Has her military not torn down the Stars and
Stripes too many times for us to think that she has respect for it?
I am sure that you are aware of the fact that there is no open door
where Japan controls. Witness Korea, Manchukwo, etc. I agree with
those who are saying that if Japan wins this war and holds China as a
vassal, as she is holding Korea for instance, then she will use the
machinery our citizens are selling her now and wit the raw materials
that she will have from China, she will be ready to turn toward the
Occident for further campaigns. You are, of course, familiar with
the "Tanaka Memorial" and you also know that in many respects Japan
is following that, step by step. Her ambitions will not be satisfied
with Asiatic dominance.
I have said wnough and perhaps too much. I must beg your pardon
for intruding upon your time. As I told you when I called to see you,
I have nothing to put over, no "axe to grind", to use a good old Tenn-
essee term. I am simply wantingto see righteousness enthroned and
justice prevail. I have written at this length because as an Ameri-
can citizen I am chacrinned. As a citizen of the world I am wanting
peace and right to reign. I believe, sir, that you are in a position
to lead our people. I am convinced that the people as a whole are in
sympathy with China and her cause of right. I is only a small minor-
ity who are carrying on this business and bringing disgrace to our
nation. The people will support you.
Thanking you again and with ev ry good wish, I am
Very truly yours,
Juo.cStawl.
John C. Hawk,

Dear Mr. Hull:
Departren of
I have just read a pamphlet entitled, "America's Share in Japan's
War Guilt," and have been deeply impressed by the clear-cut state-
ments of a great many outstanding leaders, including yourself, as
well as declarations of responsible organizations in regard to
America's support of Japan in her immoral invasion of China. I
am amazed and encouraged to find such deep and widespread feeling
894.24/334
over the way American corporations are making possible the pro-
longation of this bloody war. It would seem that with all of
this sentixent, there should be some way by which it could be
made articulate. Is there no way whereby proper steps could
be taken to halt this traffic in death and destruction between
899.
America and Japan? Do you have any suggestion as to what in-
dividual American citizens or groups of citizens could and
should do to withhold from the Japanese militarists the support
that is now being given them by American business?
I believe, as you do, that the real hope of China lies in the
Christianisation of her life and culture. Undoubtedly the
present practice of professedly Christian nations, like the
United States in their economic support of Japan in her pagan
and ruthless aggression will make it exeeedingly difficult for
the Chinese people to develop or retain a confident and re-
ceptive attitude toward the Christian faith. From the stand-
point of every high interest in life, to have such a thing
happen is tragedy.
In the face of this situation, do you have a word of counsel
F/A
or guidance with reference to a practical approach toward
FILED
the correction of it? I shall greatly appreciate whatever
help you may give.
Very respectfully yours,
5. Raymond Alleta
Field Representative for
Christian Education

Aug. 30, 1938.
Dear Mr. Maloney:
Without undue sympathy to either Japan or China, may
I urge you to do something to ston the flow of American
supplies 1ncluding munitions, planes, scrap iron, etc.,
to Japan? It is difficult to maintsin American self-
respect, not to mention a Christian conscience, when it
1s so obvious that the slaughter of innocent victims
goes on because we Americens furnish the war materials.
Surely there are other ways of aiding business and fur-
nishing Jobs than at the cost of human blood on such a
wholesale basis. If Oriental or other wars cannot con-
tinue without our suppli s, then that blood is on our hands.
Respectfully yours,
HERMAN BORNE,
2713 Jol1et St., New Orleans, La.

AUG: 1938
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
Dear Sir:--
alh9-2-38
RVN/amn
It seems incredible that the sentiment of the people of the United
States is practically unanimous against the atttitude of the Japanese
militarists in China, while at the same time our steel and oil and
copper compamies and dealers in scrap iron continue supplying the ma-
terials to Japan for brutal aggression.
Are there no moral obligations for steel and oil corporations; or,
if not, can they not be made amenable to the public conscience? The
government of the Wnited States is certainly the proper medium for ex-
sale to foreign buyers, just as it does the sale of lethal weapons to
894.24
individuals in this country.
2
Mrs. Hinman and I have recently returned from China. We may refer
the threat of amrial bombing. We know how the Chinese trust the Amer-
ican people in spite of the damning evidence that much of Japanese killo
ing machinery comes from America. How long will conscienceless corpo-
rations defy public sentiment, and scatter death abroad as they would
not dare to do at home? We call on our government to end this defiance
of the "good neighbor policy" by those who want to sell a little more
oil and steel to international brigands.
It will not be necessary to supply war materials to China in their
struggle for life and independence if our government supplements a con-
trol of American exports toJapan with theutmost in diplamabic pressure
against those who have made themselves the world's outlaws. Courageous
dofiance of Japan's policy in China by diplomatic representations can
do much to check her mad career, provided we implement American public

MSHM Dn tyne
DEPARTCE
Secrotary
PETITION TO BE SUBMITTED TO THE
38
PM: 37 PRESIDENT OF THE UNITED STATES ON THE
ach9-3-38
JAPANESE INVASION OF CHINA
september 6 1938.
RVtt/amn
WHEREAS, the United States of America and many other nations have
indioted Japan as the violator of the Nine-Power Treaty and the Kellogg
Pact, in the present Sino-Japanese conflict, fomented by the Military
Party of Japan, and has repeatedly protested against the bombings of un-
defended Chinese cities and the unnecessary slaughter of Chinese non-
lyo
combatants, and
WHEREAS, we feel put to shame that American raw materials are be
ing used to manufacture weapons of war for Japan, American planes are
being used to carry American-made bombs for the destruction of Chinese
civilian population, American shells are loading Japanese guns, and
America is supplying to Japan huge quantities of scrapiron, oil, trucks
and other materials used in the furtherance of the war, over and above
the amounts of our normal trade of recent years, and
WHEREAS, we sympathize with the Chinese people in their desire to
894.24/342
build up an independent, democratic state, an end gravely threatened by
894.24
the present aggression of Japan, and deplore the traffic which makes it
possible for certain American interests to profit from the wanton de-
struction of the innocent victims of Japanese aggression.
THEREFORE, we the undersigned respectfully petition the President
of the United States, in accordance with the principle of his Good
Neighbor enunciation, and in the interest of the people of the United
States, China and Japan, to take every practical means, direct of in-
direct, to bring to an end a traffic from our cuuntry which is com-
pelling us to be a partner in the destruction of the Chinese people.
And we desire to go on record as expressing our solidarity with seamen,
longshoremen and all other workers and labor organizations which refuse
to handle munitions, war supplies and raw materials destined for Japan,
and throw our moral support behind financial, industrial and other in-
terests which refuse to lend money, extend credit or sell war materials
to Japan.
O.R Jarwater
F/HR
will bredig
Mr. t Mra. Auderaon Keeble

Dear Mr. President,
CORDS
Department of State
Every day we hear of Japanese bombs murdering hundreds of
Chinese women and little children and men. Every day we read of American
exports of crude oil, gasoline, kerosine, scrap iron, corner and many
other things that carry the bombing planes and that go into the bombs
that kill the Chinese, and that fill the hospitals there of kind missionary
4
doctors. The gold which Japan is sending us to pay for these things is
of no use to us, but is simply buried here with all the other surplus
gold.
89
We feel somehow guilty of abetting this awful wickedness, but we
feel so helpless about it. Do you also feel that way, Mr. President?
24/343
It seems to many of us that you could stop this flow of munitions, auto
trucks, and war materials to Japan. Do try it, Mr President, and you
will have the backing of a large majority of the citizens of our land.
I am in hearty agreement with the American Committee for
Non-participation in Japanese Aggression, 3 West 4Oth Street, New York,
as given in its pamphlot, "AMERICA'S SHARE IN JAPAN'S WAR GUILT."

Dear Sir:
For ever a year Japan has been conducting her latest
campaign in China. I believe I am correct in saying that during
this time the United States exports of war materiels to Japan
have increased enormously, and that we are new mupplying her
more munitions, sora iron, and other producte than any other
ceuntry, perhaps than all ethers cembined. It appears te me
994.24/545
that we are cantributint in this way toward prolonation of
a war that is indefensible, hcwover cne looks at it. Aside
frem meral ccusideratians I feel that the United States is
jeopardizing her friendly relations and commercial prespects
for the future in China and the Orient generally.
I should, therefore, like te protest against our
furnishing oredit te Japan, and against aiding and abetting
her in other ways. In view of Japan's attitude toward American
nationals, and American intereats in China, I feel the United
States hould adept a stiffer pelicy all along the line. The
fiction that a war is net a war because it is undeclared be-
comes daily more ridiculcus.
Having lived in China, my pre-Chinese sentiments are
streng, and no doubt my understanding of the diplematio in-
tricacies of eur position in the Orient is weak. Therefere,
fer my ewn interest and information, I should appreciate
receiving any public reperts or statements of pelicy frem
your department that might be pertinent.
Rospectfully yours,

Hon. Cordell Hull

894.24/346
My dear Mr. Secretary Hull:
I have been reading with very great iaterest,
and even greater approval, some statements made by you and reported
by the press with reference to American foreigh policies following
the Munich accord. Like a good many Americans, I am deeply inter-
ested in the post-Munich conditions, particularly with reference to
the situation in the Far East.
May I express the hope that some more effective
plan of action b our Government may be taken preventing the ship-
ment of war supplies to Japan. I have nothing to offer in the vay
of suggestion, and if I had I am not sure that I would make suc'h to
you as I am not familiar with all the conditions that would have to
be considered in connection with any change of policy.
I have seen hints in the press of a declaration
of wer by China which would make the declaration of the Neutrality
Act on the part of our Government possible. In view of the fact
that all of the Chinese ports that might be used by that country for
the receipt of war materials are now in the hands of the Japanese,
application of the Neutrality Act now would affect Japan more than
China.
I want to express my own very deep personal
appreciation for the note which your department dispatched to Japan
sometime in September. It seems to me that the position taken by
you is eminently sound and I trust lays the foundation for a practi-
cal means of putting to an end the help which we have been giving,
however unwillingly, to Japan in the war against China.
I shall watch with interest the reply of the
Japanese Government and the note which your own department may make
when this reply has been received.
Very truly yours,
ECPitess
F/WB

September 1, 1938
dear Mr. Yost:
Department of Staie
Tiappreclate your very courteous letter with the statenents regurdi goth
President should not declare that a state of war exists between Chine and Japan.
It seems such a quibble and the attemnt to deceive himself or the people is a failure.
Certainly a "state of war" exists betwee: China and Janan whethor the President chooses
to make the statement or not.
894.24/352
I do not believe that decention is needed to keep this country out of war. My
894.2
personal opinion may be of little value, but the opinion of the people of the nation
will decide some questions which belong to them, rather than to the President.
I have been gratified by the statements made by Secretury Hull, but he has no power
under this arrangement to do more than politely state his opinion. I have a great
respect and regard for Secretary Hull und only wish that he had the power that has
been given to the President. That power belongs to Congress and it was, as many
feared, a mistake to transfer it.
2
Much depends on your definition of a "Belligerent Nation". Is it not a war making or
fighting nation? Does it apply to a person or nation defending his life against a
marderous attack?
Evasions and deceptions will not keep this country out of war. A firm hand, an out-
spoken statement by the people which might be secured through a referendum submitted
to the people would put them in the position of responsibility assumed by the
Executive. A strong statement by the People backed by consistent embargo would do
more to check Japan and other dangerous neighbors than diplomatic statements based on
deception.
The present situation in Europe is, many believe, due to the weak and cowardly attitude
of England's Prime Minister. Now that England has spoken we may see an end to Germany's
bluff. The United States should stand for right, not with a political league but on
her own responsibility.
5
If this Government would stop sending or lending to an aggressor nation, there would
be hope. China, under attack, should have out-spoken defense by every decent nation.
The present situation of bluff in the world can only be met by a courageous decisive
stand which will prevent war rather than encourage it.
Many thanks for the statements you so kindly sent. I would not presume to give my own
point of view, if I stood alone. I do, however, know that many feel as I do that it
F/HR

=== Page 276 === (confidence 0.96)
-2-
Y
time to do something more than has been done. Note this last protest from
Issionaries in China.
My experience as Chairman of the Committee to secure the funds to build three colleges
for women in China; Ginling, Yenching and Shanghai Medical, has given me more than
a passing interest and knowledge of Chine.
My complaint is not against Secretary Hull, but against a bed move on the part of
Congress and lack of action on the purt of the President who was given power.
Sincerely yours
Luag o- Brabrdy

Dear Sir:
AND RECORDS
844.24
The enclosed clipping from the editorial page of the Phila-
delphia Record embodies statements which I emphatically endorse.
A recent survey by the Foreign Policy Association shows that
54.4 % of the means for carrying on the war in China by the Japanese
has been supplied by the United States. And yet, in the face of all
this support given to Japan, you continue to protest the use of this
material for aggressive action. Within the past week you have renew-
ed a general indictment of nations who wilfully ignore treaties and
openly disregard internatiunal law. And at the same moment you must
be conscious of violating the spirit and intent of our own Neutrality
894.24/354
Act. Is your department unwillins at this late date to acknowledge
the errors in the conduct of our foreign relations, or does it pro-
pose to continue to stultify itself by pursuing a policy inconsistent
with and in direct contravention of the will of this nation as ex-
pressed in the Neutrality Act?
Mr. Joseph Green, Chief of the Munitions Control Section of
your Department has revealed to me the very insubstantial grounds on
which the action of the President and yourself have been acting in
this matter. Mr. Green insists that your department could not act
otherwise than has been done, because the President has not "found"
that a state of war exists either in Spain or China! I am inter-
ested to know just how he or you would define the present state of
affairs in both thosecountries, if it is not a state of war in fact?
Now, Mr. Green also states that the Neutrality Act and the
amendment thereto relating to the Spanish situation, state unequivo-
22
cally that "should the President find a state of war to exist between
two or more foreign states, he would be obliged to proclaim such fact
and that, following such proclamation, the embargo upon shipment of
munitions and certain specified materials, such as copper, cotton
etc. would be required by the Act.
Recently we were advised that Mr. Johnson, Assistant Secretary
of War to study the feasibility of building a military road throughout
Alaska and with a view to secure a free passage through British Col-
umbia of troops and military supplies. Also it is known that our
western coast defenses are being strengthened and the the naval squad
ron enlarged. Obviously all this preparation as well as that in the
F
Hawaian Islands and elsewhere in the Pacific, are designed to check ,
Japanese aggression. The nation is thus subjected to vast ex ense fo
"defense" purposes at the same time that we are equiping our prospect
方
enemies with the means to conduct an aggressive war, which if success-
ful will undermine democratic institutions throughout the world.
cliffing
Very truly yours,
FEHyolof.

=== Page 286 === (confidence 0.99)
LD OUT 9/8/38
has been sold down the river.
people, thank God, have had no
was a day when our people should
to be clear of a British alliance, today is
possible, as rumors indicate, that our State
rtment may have played a subtle role in
ing the British force the Czechs to knuckle
der to Hitler, by granting concessions in the
Sudeten area which virtually mean its sever-
ance, both political and cultural, from Czecho-
slovakia.
But the State Department as at present con-
stituted does not represent the American people.
We only hope the Roosevelt Administration will
recognise that fact—before it is too late.
Perfidious Albion!
If that famous phrase isn't being echoed and
re-echoed in Czechoslovakia tonight, then the
Czechs are an amazing people.
The peace of Europe, of course, is preserved.
For the moment.
But how much longer can the British expect
to work their magic formula of preserving peace
by giving away chunks of other countries?
The whole Franco-British setup in Eastern Eu-
rope, the whole basis of the Little Entente, is
based upon the notion of military insurance.
Rumania, Jugoslavia, Poland, the rest of the
smaller nations all have hugged the British orbit
for one reason:
Protection.
British policy now is made clear as offering no
protection whatever, but, instead, as utilizing
those small nations as pawns on His Majesty's
chessboard.
Ethiopia relied on the British. Ethiopia no
longer exists.
Austria relied on Britain. Austria is no more.
Czechoslovakia has relied on Britain. And now
she, too, comes to appreciate the finesse of Cham-
berlain in "making sacrifices" for peace.
Obviously, such a system will have to come
to an end. Either the British will run out of
smaller nations to sacrifice—
Or the smaller nations will run out on the
British, which is much more likely, and place
their insurance policies with Hitler, Mussolini &
Co., instead of Chamberlain, Halifax, Ltd.
Once the smaller nations become convinced
that they can hope for nothing by banking on
the British, they will hasten, as Hungary seems
to be doing, to make a deal with the dictators.
With no satellite countries left to sacrifice.
what could Chamberlain do?
If it were suffciently necessary to selfish Brit-
ish interests, Britain would fight. But if things
came to that pass, she also would do what she
has done for centuries.
She would hunt some other nation to do most
of the fighting for her.
Already the British Dominions have emitted
rumblings of protest at the notion that they be
expected to fead the mother country's advance
in a new conflict.
But if not the Colonies. there's still Uncle
Sam.
Already the British Government counts upon
our State Department to do its bidding. Al-
ready, Chamberlain has inquired whether we
would care to repeat our sucker role in the
World War.
With the memory of the sacrifice of Czecho-
slovakia fresh in our minds-let us make clearer
than ever that WE'RE STAYING OUT
Let John Bull fight his ewn battles. sacrifice
his own possessions.
And if YOU feel that way let it be known at
Washington.

My ear Mr. Green,
3
I have just read again an interesting letter from you of July
874.74/229

3
26193
you
grote so fully.
I recognize that in the matter of arms, ammunition and imple-
ments of war alone, it was true as you said that more had been shipped
894.24/3
to China than to Japan during the period from July 1937 to July 1938.
This, however, takes no account of the vast amount of other material
essential for the prosecution of war which Japan has been securing from
the United States, such as for example, scrap iron and oil. I think
it is undisputed that Japan would hardly be able to prosecute her present
iniquitous course in China without the materials which go to her from
this country. I am aware that our present legislation makes it difficult
for the President or the Department of Stete to do anything about this.
At the same time, there is a humiliating inconsistency for the President
aqgressor
to speak of boycotting nations, and for the Secretary of State to make
his condemnation of these nations plain, when in fact, we go on being
the accessories in what we condemn. In addition to its words, which seem
to be quite right and true, is there anything which the State Department
F/WB
is planning to recommend and to try to accomplish to the end that America
may no longer be a partner with Japan in her aggression?
Sincerely yours,
Mr. Joseph C. Green, Chief

of accomplishments.

the Japanese on the Chinese is mainly supported through war
material by people in the United States.

this frightful situation which certainly cannot be justified
from Christian or humane standpoints.

the material by which Japanese outrages in Chinese territory
are made pcasible? Certainly to shut off the supoly of
material wiil bring the undeclared war to a close.
Earnestly hoping for your pronounced leadership in this
item of worldwide interest, I am, with sincerest good wishes,
Very truly yours,
WM. DANNER

Hankew, Central China
July 26,1938.
was eme of the refreshments of mind and soul after a terrible
