yeu ia Kashmir A The yery thought of it seems like a werld apart
all this war herrer 1%
After some weeks of rain and no raißs,we have had a fortnight ef
shestly raids. One very near us last Tuesday with a theusand casualties
amengst the peer and anether une that struak very near the Ameriean Church
Missiem Hespital in Muohang across the river Yangste. I went moress to
see if I could hely Miss Reiley there. A maternity hospital opened by
the Gevernment was struak. The bomb wert through three fleors of reim-
foseed cemerete,killing or wounding all the patients in it. Seme ef the
methers lay so peaceful in death with their babies clasped in their arme.
while the reseue mquad were trying to get out bedies frem beneath the de-
brise It seemed as though they were happier than theenes who were se
abdemen with a rupture of the uterus and a long slit in the buttook of
the feetus. Did ever cuch devil's work darken civilisatien before !
Last Tuesday the bombs dropped so near to us that as the bombers
pasmed over our heads, we waited in a tense stillness not knowing waat a
moment might bring. But oh, so glad to be able te rest one's heart in our
loving Lord's care and to feel Him near. Then we heard the thud, thud of 
bembs falling and then the explosions, and our hearts were beatdags:
knewing that same peor folk were being killed somewhere near. A Guild
Hall net far from us housed some hundreds of refugees from a nearby
provinee - hardly any escaped alive. From another place a stream of stret-
ehers brought in wounded to us.
Our four doctors started at once to operate,two in each roum,while I
went along the stretohers picking out those who had a chance of life to
ge next to the operating-room. Relatives were pulling at my dress implor-
Ing me to let their particular loved one go in next,leathe to accept the
feet that they were beyond human aid. Oh,it was ghastly ! The heat (we
are usually on the hills at this time, but out hills are in the battle
area and we are all needed in cur stations), the suffering so patiently
berne,the misery,the sight and smells made me feel sick at heart and in
bedy. One could only ask, "Dear Lord, how long must this terror continue!"
The next dey another raid before the poor victims of the prewious d
day had even recovered from the shock of operations and everyone was busy
with dressings.We notice that the raid victims suffer most when the air
raid sirens shriek over the city. One small bey of seven - so tiny, was
en a mall boat when a bomb struck it and killed his father and brothers
in front of him and injured his own leg, which had to be amputated. He
breaks out in a sweat and starts up crying when he hears the warnings.
Ah me ! and we try to teach the world of brotherly love !
It is a bitter thing too to see American and British names on bits
of bombs picked up. Last week some of our American missionary friends
took pieces to the Consul and he agreed that they were from Americae An
English author is here in Hankow this week and says that Britain and
Ameriea could step the war in a week if they would get together and that
America is waiting for England to take the lead. On,if she could only see
what we see syrely she would take the lead right speedily. God forgive
her if she doesn't !
All preparations have been made for the invasion of the city.The
Consular authorities want to draw off all their nationals possible,but
the missionaries have plainly stated their intention to stay. Our staff
gets lower and lower because those who can afford it are leaving for the
farther inland provinces and taking their murse daughters with them. I
cannet blame them, as untold horrors have been perpretrated on the civél-
ians left behind in cccupied cities. We have many high school girls acting
as practical aids and thus we are carrying on and intend to continue to do
so whatever comes if God keeps us.
My leave is due now. I could not rossibly leave at present how-
ever and must wait till God opens the way, which I hope may be next spring.
D.V. Our Supt. is a Chinese dootor a:d he needs his English colleagues
Tery urgently and just now I am the Senior one so could not think of lear
One of the Hankow nurses who had to visit a clinio in a much bombed
area came back one day after a raid with shaking hands and she was all
wed. The next time we said to her, "Don't go today." But she smiled
A replied,"ky Bible today said in my reading,"I will fear no evil while

After some weeks of rain and no raißs,we have had a fortnight ef

mengst the peer and anether une that struak very near the Ameriean Church
Missiem Hespital in Muohang across the river Yangste. I went moross te

feseed cenerete,killing or wounding all the patients in it. Seme ef the
methers lay so peaceful in death with their babies clasped in their armp.
while the reseue squad were trying to get out bedies frem beneath the de-

grievously wounded. One mix months pregnant woman hed a gaping wound ef 
abdemen with a rupture of the uterus and a long slit in the buttook of
the feetus. Did ever cueh devil's work darken civilisatien before !
Last Tuesday the bombs dropped so near to us that as the bombers
passed ever our heads, we waited in a tense stillness not knowing what a
moment might bring. But oh,so glad to be able to rest one's heart in our
Loving Lord's care and to feel Him near. Then we heard the thud, thud of 
bembs falling and then the explosions, and our hearts were peatdass?
knewing that some peor folk were being killed somewhere near. A Guild
Hall net far from us housed some hundreds of refugees from a nearby
provinee - hardly any escaped alive. From another place a stream of stret-
ehers brought in wounded to us.
Our four doctors started at once to operate,two in each roum,while I
went along the stretchers picking out those who had a chance of life to
ge next to the operating-room. Relatives were pulling at my dress implor-
ing me to let their particular loved one go in next,leathe to accept the
faet that they were beyond human aid. Oh,it was ghastly ! The heat (we
are usually on the hills at this time, but out hills are in the battle
area and we are all needed in cur stations), the suffering so patiently
borne,the misery,the sight and smells made me feel sick at heart and in
bedy. One could only ask, "Dear Lord, how long must this terror continue!"
The next day another raid before the poor victims of the prewious d
day had even recovered from the shock of operations and everyone was busy
with dressings.We notice that the raid victime suffer most when the air
raid sirens shriek over the city. One small bey of seven - so tiny, was
on a mall boat when a bomb struck it and killed his father and brothers
in front of him and injured his own leg, which had to be amputated. He
breaks out in a sweat and starts up crying when he hears the warnsngs.
Ah me ! and we try to teach the world of brotherly love !
It is a bitter thing too to see American and British names on bits
of bombs picked up. Last week some of our American missionary friends
took pieces te the Consul and he agreed that they were from America An
English author is here in Hankow this week and says that Britain and
Ameriea could step the war in a week if they would get together and that
America is waiting for England to take the lead. On,if she could only see
what we see syrely she would take the lead right speedily. God forgive
her if she doesn't !
All preparations have been made for the invasion of the city.The
Consular authorities want to draw off all their nationals possible,but
the missionaries have plainly stated their intention to stay. Our staff
gets lower and lower because those who can afford it are leaving for the
farther inland provinces and taking their nurse daughters with them. I
cannet blame them, as untold horrors have been perpretrated on the civél-
ians left behind in occupied cities. We have many high school girls acting
as practical aids and thus we are carrying on and intend to continue to do
so whatever comes if God keeps us.
Ny leave ts due now. I could not rossibly leave at present how-
ever and must wait till God opens the way, which I hope may be next spring.
D.V. Our Supt. is a Chinese dootor and he needs his English colleagues
Tery urgently and just now I am the Senior one so could not think of lear

wed. The next time we said to her, "Don't go today." But she smiled
replied,"My Bible today said in my reading,"I will fear no evil while

Dear Mr. Hull:
After watching for a year the course
of the undeclared war in China we the undersigned
have become convinced that its continuing tragedy
is being made possible largely by the unrestrained
flow of American munitions and war supplies to
Japan.
We believe that Japan's action in China
is in direct contradiction to the principles and
values for which we as a democratic nationg stand,
and to which you in your published addresses have
given such discerning and accurate statement. Therefore
we urge that appropriate action be taken as soon
as possible to bring about cessation of our aid
to the aggressor nation.
Clarence H. Hamilton

It is possibly sending coels to Newcastle to add anything

"owever, as cheirmen of the Committee Against International
Aggression, I wish to express to you the sentiment of the people
in this part of the country as well as my own personel opinion.
We feel that continued shinments of munitions of wer,
machinery for their manufecture and the materiels fron which they
ere mede is a cisgrace to the merchents of our country, as well
as to the nation as a whole.
e, by treaty end by sacred pledge, would pretend to be
friends of China and ageinst the aggressor, Janen. Yet we con-
tinually injure our friendly nation and aid the eggressor nation
and one might easily say without possibility of doubt, merica's
potential if not active enemy; the most destructive agent of
world peace today.
Your citizens believe that if you wish to stop this traffic
a letter from you to these merchants of death, or a radio mes-
sage to your people, would stop the traffic overnight.
Some weeks ago a letter was sent to you suggesting that mow
the United States government should notify Japan that no money
would be loaned to her for the development of the territories
she is stealing from her neighbor, and that our nation would not
in future recognize any territories so acquired. Thousands of
your people are convinced that this action should be taken "now".
In these days of international perfidy as exhibited by the
British and Frenoh treatment of Czeohoslovakia, honest people
the world over stand aghast. It seems that the solemn obliga-
tions of the statesmen of the world and the nations they repre-
sent is of fer less value than a screp of paper. It is so pu-
trid that the stench rises to high heaven.

Dear Frank:
The enclosed letter to Jecretary Hull vas signed by
fifteen persons living on the compound of the issionary Iome
where I have been staying since January. Ve received a routine
acknowledgment from the State Department and personal acknowledgments
894.24/376
from the two Massachusetts senators.
Secretary Hull's broadcast last evening was magnificent.
That is the stand which I am convinced the vast majority of our
countrymen want the State Department and the Administration to
take in these days when the future of oivilization literally
9
trembles in the balance.
But is it not necessary to "implement" our oft-repeated
politico-economio creed by concrete deeds? In particular, what
is there to prevent some such action as is suggested in our letter
of June 20 regarding the export of the sinews of war to Japan.
not
"
11
Would that be taking sides? some timorous soul might ask. Most
assuredly it would! How in the world can we refrain from taking
sides? Who wants to tixxta remain neutral in suoh a clear-out
F
choioe as the Seoretary himself so eloquently described for us
/A
last night?
"We might inour the displeasure of Japan." I reply,
"So what?"
Sinoe when have we been afraid of inourring the
displeasure of Japan? To be sure, our own record in international
oollaboration sinoe the War has not been spotless. We did muoh to
insure that the League of Nations should be still-born. However, in
the present instanoe it is Japan who is ohiefly responsible for

=== Page 378 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
the appalling and staggering disintegration in international
relations, I feel. That be ing so, why should we, at this
moment, be aiding and abetting this conscienceless aggressor by
supplying her with the wherewithal to continue her conquest of
China?
What is there to prevent a request from the State
Department for absolute and inmediate cessation of all export from
America of any and all materials which contribute to keeping
Japan's war-machine going? And, after such warning, what is
there to prevent the publication of a list of any firms or
individuals which persist in this demoralizing trade?
In one
word, I think, my dear Frank, that we as a people and as a govern-
ment have already stated our position clearly enough and repeatedly
enough. The whole world -- including the "bandit" nations -- know
that position quite well. The only thing they are interested in
is the question, Is this just one more pious sermon, or is the
United States at last sufficiently aroused to do something about it,--
something which will really hurt their cause?
It seems perfectly
clear to me (perhaps in my ignorance) that the Italo-German-Japanese
combination is and has been throwing one bluff after another, until
they have had the peaceful, decent-thinking demooracies stymied and
on the continuous run--backward. Czeohoslovakia, on May 21, showed
the way to meet that sort of bluster and bluff. They called it.
Why should we -- great, powerful, inpregnable -- be less ready to
baok our professions with some concrete deeds than little Czeoho-
slovakia?
Most sinoerely yours,
Paul Rowland

=== Page 383 === (confidence 0.94)
WHEREAS, the United States of America and many other nations
have indicted Japan as the violator of the Nine-Power Treaty
and the Kellogg Pact, in the present Sino-Japanese conflict,
fomented by the Military Party of Japan, and have repeatedly
protested against the bombings of undefended Chinese cities
and the unnecessary slaughter of Chinese non-combatants, and
WHEREAS, we feel put to shame that American raw materials are
being used to manufacture weapons of war for Japan, American
planes are being used to carry American-made bombs for the
destruction of Chinese civilian population, American shells
are loading Japanese guns, and America is supplying to Japan
huge quantities of scrap-iron, oil, trucks and other materials
used in the furtherance of the war, over and above the amounts
of our normal trade of recent years, and
WHEREAS, we sympathize with the Chinese people in their desire
to build up an independent, democratic state, an end gravely
threatened by the present aggression of Japan, and deplore the
traffic which makes it possible for certain American interests
to profit from the wanton uestruction of the innocent victims
of Japanese aggression,
THEREFORE, we the undersigned respectfully petition the Presi-
dent of the United States, in accordance with the principle of
his Good Neighbor enunciation, and in the interest of the
people of the United States, China and Japan, to take every
practicable means, airect or indirect, to bring to an end a
traffic from our country which is compelling us to be a part-
ner in the destruction of the Chinese people.
Suricl E. Gray
Brealie B. litdeni
Enily Sila.

concerned about America's share in Japan's war guilt.

Japan could not obtain elsewhere, makes America the
virtual partner of Japan in her bestial war on China.

with you 1n the endeavor to stop the United States from

RESCLUTIONS
1.
Relative to War Materials
Whereas the United States of America and other nations have repeat-
edly protested against t'e bombings of undefended cities and the unnecessary
slaughter of non-combatant civilians and children, and
Whereas we feel put to shame that Anerica has continued to supply a
substantial portion of the war materials whicl help to make possible such
outrageous attacks upon defenseless civili ns and children, and
Whereas the discontinuance of the sale of such materials would do
much to restore a sense of national responsibility and national morality
in international affairs even ti ough the surrender of profits therefrom
woul involve sacrifice on the p rt of many in America.
Therefore, be it resolved:
(1) That ve respectfully petition the Presicent of the United
States, in accordonce with the principle of ris good Yei  bor enunci tion,
and in the interests of t'e people of the Waited states to t ke vary
practical aeans, irect or inirect, to bring to an end  tr rfic from our
country which is compelling us to be  purtner in the destruction of other
peoples' nation.l independence nd liberty.
(2) That a copy of t ese resolutiona be sent to t e President of
the United States und to  e Secretary oi itate, also to tne local papers.
No. 2. Relative to Ciina Relief
Phe Red Jross Campai m for ina relief terainated June 15 vit
very mediocre recu's in shite of Jhina's unprecedente neers. Pe Jhurch
cannot be the Church and rem in in ifierent to this cistress. It mas,
therefore, rormed the Jureh Jomittee tor Jina telier by appointing
reoresentatives from the t ree constituent roups of  ort: Aierica, the
Federal Jouncil of  urc es, te orei n : ission Conicrence, und the
Crina Famine Relief, for the purpose of ruisiny funds for China relief.
Therefore, be it resolved tat ve cooperute in this nation-wide
effort to awaken America to her sense of moral obligation to Jhina's suffer-
ing millions and that as indivicuals ve ccert responsibility for nelping
arouse interest un securing ifts in our local communities. "In as much
as ye have done it unto one of the least of ti ese ye have done it unto me."
No. 3.
.Relative to Ladras
We are pleased to learn of the Jorld : issionary Co. ference vhich
is to convene at Madras, India, Decem er 13-30, 1938, when representatives
from every part of Christencom will be assembled for prayer and a restudy
of the Great Commission in the liglt of tre present world orer. e learn
with pleasure also t?at a series of post maras conferences are plunned
for Canada and the United States.
Therefore, be it resolved that we join our fellow Christiuns
around the world in prayer that this gat! ering at Ladras may find new light

FAM LASTENN AFFAIRS
Dear Mr. President:
For a long time I have wanted to write you but I feel so
insignificant to trespass upon your time. I have wanted you to
know that even if I am so small a bit of this large world, with all
my heart I believe in you and your honest and great desire for
justice and the best good for all. Your duties and responsihilities
894.24/380
are so overwhelming - such terrific pressure goes with that awful
office - the criticism so narrow, severe, cruel, but with it all,
to quote Mr. Hugh S. Johnson (!) * * you are such a "thoroughbred".
I am not bit enough nor know enough to know what to do,
but there are so many cruel things to be righted and this effort
of a letter to you is to ask your help in this brutal thing Japan
is doing in China. Can we longer remain indifferent to the great
tragedy of our times? Do we as Americans not bear partial
responsibility for the fact that Japan has pretty much all the
supplies she needs?
After Secretary of State Cordell Hull's anti-war statement
of August 27, the Baltimore Sun quotes Premier Mussolini's newspaper
answer: "Not a day passes without bringing from America a sermon
whose argument is always the same. While the United States has the
first place as an exporting country for pacifist speeches, it has
the first place also as exporter of armaments. Our Japanese friends
know something about that. As Japanese they receive sermons from
America; as customers they get from the same America whatever they
need to meet the situation in China - for pay naturally."
China is making a magnificent struggle for liberty. The
least we can do is to withhold aid from her enemy. Can you not, Mr.
President, refuse absolutely our supporting this war of aggression?
F/A
Sincerely and respectfully yours,
(Miss) Alice E. Frame

Dear Mr. President:
894.24/383
Each day's delay means more women and children
894.
killed or maimed, and these are counted in thousands;
more men civilians destroyed by the narcotic drugs
deliberately provided very cheaply by Japan; to say
mothing of the thousands killed or wounded on the
battlefiolds of China.
While this goes on,our country, more than any
other, supplies Japan the materials with which to com-
mit these atrocities. I wish you could have heard the
speech of Mr. Wellington Koo in the Assembly of the
League of Nations last Friday; it would have moved you
to action. How long will the wretches who sell scrap-
iron to Japan be permitted to batten on the blood of
China? For some time we forbade the sale of helium
gas to ermany. Why cannot we forbid scrap-iron to
Japan? The military leaders of Japan are planning
to destroy one of the finest nations on this earth.
Must we as a nation be obliged to assist in this?
You, who are thehope of the world, cannot afford
to remain inactive. You have already done much, but
more remains to be done.
C0
The whole worlu awaits your action.

1938 SEP 24AM 910
The President of the United States
The White House
MMJNICATIONS
Washington, D. C.
AND RECORDS
Dear Mr. President:
It seems evident that we Americans are be-
coming increasingly conscious as a people of the fact
that Japan's war machine would soon be stalled with-
894.24
out American aid in the form of financial credits and
war materials. As a result of this realization, people
894.24/386
are discussing ways and means whereby this distressing
partnership might b, terminated. Truly we share at
present in Japan's war guilt.
There seems to be no douut as to your own
views regarding Japanese aggression in China and the
violation by Japan of solemn treaties to which both
the United States and Japan are parties. It is our
humble opinion that the people of America generally
are coming to share your views and to demand action.
The time seems ripe for measures by our government to
prohibit the export of war supplies to Japan.
It is our earnest hope that you will see fit
to back such measures at the next session of the Congress.
If as chief executive you were able to discover under
existing laws a direct and quicker method for dealing
with the problem, you would undoubtedly find popular
support.  The people of Japan are not our enemies, but
we are determined to have no share in the ruthless
aggression of the Japanese military clique in China.
Sincerely yours,
Henng N Wo0000
F/A
FAR EASTERN ALTAINS
SEP29 1938
Henry H. Welles

Washington, D. C.
My dear Mr. Hull:
I have admired and supported the position of the
administration as to international affairs. I an wondering
if the state department should not encourage the taking of
immediate steps to stop the shipment of all arms and war
material to Japan.
The statement is abroad and voiced freely by
Chinese authorities that Japan could not continue this war
without this aid given by the United States.
8
6
Moreover, it has been asserted that most of these
94.2
shipments of war materials and accessories are made on long
term credits.
24/38
This, if true, seems to us to be in effect aiding
Japan in its unjust, inhuman, and arbitrary military aim
to conquer China by force.it is said that 31,000,000
Chinese refugees have fled from East China to West China
7
before the ravages of Japanese militarism.
Are we nationally culpable in this matter ? I
believe in national idealism and do not think it is
justifiable for the sake of profit to prejudice the cause
of a people who are fighting for deliverance from what you
have weli called international highwaymen. Please remember
that the God of nations still lives and w ll bless America
as she befriends the weak in their fight against oppression
and tyranny.
I have the honor to be,
FAR 1
~IlAINS
Cordially yours,
Frank m. Silaley
F/A
President of the Evanston, Illinois
Ministers' Association
(An Organization of Sixty Members)

Dear Mr. Secretary:
Allow me to express my deep appreciation
of the statement in your address at Fashville on June third
and other similar statements reg:rding the use of armed force
to attain national ends through aggression and aggrandizement.
Your statement is very true that such force "is being employed
with brutality and savagery that outrage and shock every humane
instinct".
In making this statement, you doubtless had
in mind the aggressive warfare of Japan in China, and it is my
hope that you may find the means to prevent America's partnership
in this aggression and brutality by the sale of munitions and
raw materials to Japan.
Very truly yours,

Dear Sir:
Secretary to the President
We of the Christian family on the
Pacific Coast, members of the congregation of
the Pasadena Presbyterian Church, resenting the
wanton slaughter of non-combatants by Japanese
in China, and conscious that American made planes
and munitions aid the aggressor in that undeclared
war, respectfully petition the President of these
United States, in accordance with his enunciated
Good Neighbor policy, to take every practicable
step to put moral standards above financial
considerations and end a traffic wherein our people
have become profitable partners in the destruction
of the Chinese.
Very truly yours,

me
Seoretary to the President
The Board of Managers of our Society meeting in uarterly ses ion today
passed the following resolution unanimously, and as Recording Secretary of the
Board I was instructed to forward it to you:
H, the United States of nerica and many other nations have
indicted Japan as the violator of the Nine-Power Treaty and the Kellog
Fact, in the present Sino-Japanese conflict, fomented by the Military
Party of Japan, and have repeatedly protested against the bombings of
undefended Chinese cities and the unnecessary slaughter of Chinese non-
combatants, and
894.24/400
MR, we feel put to shame that American raw materials are
being used to manufacture weapons of war for Japan, merican planes are
being used to carry American-made bombs for the destruction of Chinese
civilian population, merican shells are loading Japanese guns, and
America is supplying to Japan huge quantities of scrap-iron, oil, trucks
and other materials used in the furtherance of the war, over and above
the amounts of our normal trade of recent years, and
HR, we sympathize with the Chinese people in their desire to
build up an independent, democratic state, an end gravely threatened by
the present aggression of Japan, und deplore the traffic which nakes it
possible for certain merican interests to profit from the wanton de-
struction of the innocent victims of Japanese aggression,
THER FORE, we the undersigned respectfully petition the President
of the United States, in accordance with the principle of his Good Neigh-
bor ennunciation, and in the interest of the people of the United States,
China and Japan, to take every practicable means, direct and indirect, to
bring to an end a traffic from our country which is compelling us to be a
partner in the destruction of the Chinese people.
Our Board of lianagers consists of twenty-four members representing over
500 churches and a constituency of more than 30,O00 people in the United States.
F
Trusting that you may receive divine wisdom and guidance in these trying
days, we are,
Very cordially and prayerfully yours,

"HOSPITAL OF UNIVERSAL LOVE"
(Methodist General Hospital)
Hankow: China.
July 26,1938.
It was one of the refreshments of mind and soul after a terrible week
 get a good mail on Sunday. ..... Oh, how I would like to have been with
you in Kashmir ! The very thourht of it seems like a world apart from all
this war horror !
After some weeks of rain and no raids, we have had a fortnight of
ghastly raids. One very near us last Tuesday with a thousand casualties a-
mongst the poor and another one that struck very near the American Church
Mission Hospital in Wuchang across the river Yaneste. I went across to see
if I could help Miss Reiley there, A maternity hospital opened by the Gov-
ernment was struck. The bomb went through three floors of reinforced con-
crete,killing or wounding ell the patients in it. Some of the mothers lay
so peaaeful in death witl thir bahies clacped in their arms,vile the res-
cue squad were trying to get bodies out from beneath the debris. It seumed
as thou h they were happier than the oues whe were so grievously wound d.
One six months pregnant woman had a garing wounl of th abdomen with rupture
of the uterus and a long clit in the buttock of tl footus. Did ever such
devil's work darken civilazaticn bofor!
Last Tuesday the bombe drorped so near to us that ao the bonbers
passed over our heads, we wait a in a tense stillnenr not kmowing what a mon
ent micht bring. But onso glad to be able to rost one's heart in our lov-
ing Father's care and to Ceel Fin near. Thun we huard the thud, thud of bombs
falling and then the explosions and our hearts wore vounding,knowing that
some poor folk were bein killud vomevn r near. A luild Hall not far from
us housed some hundred: of refuugoes from c nearby province - hardly any es-
caped alive. From another pluce a trcaaa of stretclers broucht in wounded
to us. Our four doctors stirted at once to op rate,two in cach room while I
went along the stretchers piclin: out thore who had a chance of life to go
noxt to the operating-room. olutives w re pulline at my drcss imploring me
to let their particular lovd one o in auxt,loathe to accept the fact that
they were beyond human aid. Oh, it was shastiy  The heat (we are usually
on the hills at this time but our hills are in th, battle area and we are
all needed in our stations),the suffering so patiently borne, the misery,
the sight and smells made me feul sick at heart and in body. One could only
ask,"Dear Lord,how long must this terro? continue !"
The next day another raid bofore the poor victims of the previous day
had even recovered from the shock of operations and everyone was busy with
dressings. We notice that the raid victims suffer most when the air raid
sirens shriek over th city. One small boy of scven - so tiny, was on a
small boat when a bomb struck it and killed hi: father and brothers in front
of him and injured his own leg,which had to be amputated. He breaks out in
a sweat and starts up crying when he huars the warnings. Ah me 1 And we try
to teach the world of brotherly love !
It is a bitter thing too to sco Anerican and British names on bits
of bombs picked up. Last week some American missionary friends took pieces
to the Consul and he agreed that they were from America. An English author
is here in Hankow this weck and says that Britain and America could stop
the war in a week if they would only get together and that Anerica is wait-
ing for England to take the lead. Oh, if she could only see what we see
surely she would take the lead right speedily. God forgive her if she docen't!
All preparations have beon made for the invasion of the city. The
Consular authorities want to draw off all their nationals possible, but the
missionaries have plainly stated their intention to stay. Our staff gets
Tower and lower because those who can afford it are leaving for the fartheryi
aland provinces and taking their nurse daughters with them. I cannot blame
them, no untold horrors have boun perpetrated on the civilians left behind
in ocoupied cities. We have many high school girls acting as practical aids
and thus we are carrying on and intund to continue to do so whatever comes
ir God kuupsus.
My lenve is due now. I could not possible leave ab present however
and ust wait till God opens the way, which I hope may be next Spring, D.V..
Our Supt. is a Chinese doctor and he needs his English collpagues vory ur-
gontly and just now I am the Senior one so could not think of leaving.
One of the Hankow nurses who had to visit a clinic in a much bombed
area came back one day after a rnid with shnking hands and she was all un-
norwed. The next time we said to her, "Don't go today." But she smiled and
repated. "My Bible today said in my reading, 'I will fear no cv/il whale
Thour art with me' ". And she went.
One of our denconesses left Anhwei, a nearby province, with a group

=== Page 479 === (confidence 0.97)
Tugees on foot in the winter snow and after an increlible journey has
recently arrived here. For weeks they hid in mountait caves where there
soarcely any food at all to be ohtained. They trekkedknkkmown ronds,
ost the way fell into swamps, wer: hurried across bridges by the soldiers
just before they blew the bridges up. Suv ral times thay hed to flee by by-
paths to avoid the enemy. had no place to sleep nights nd finally urrived
at one of our country stations more duad than alive and sczrcely recogniza-
ble. Yet now she has recovered and is luading meetings everywhere. She spoke
to our nurses' Bible class  weck or sc ago and her face shine as she said,
"I just want to witnese to the fact that the race of God is sufficient for
every need and in every circumstance of life. Never once did le fail us on
that long journey." She described how when they all had to lie in the open"
at night she reminded then of how Jusus had no place to lay Fis head and how
they all cheered up and sung hymis at the rumembrance. Also hew the Chinese
soldiers meeting them at the villages overnight asked her to tell them of
Josus and how tlad she wes to do so. This is the sort of thing we tre meet-
ing all the time and it makes our hoarts glow that out of all this horror
God is bringing a new and sweeten' life with deupened spiritual valves out
of it to this great land, and who shall say what God has for her in the fu-
ture 1
The other day I cn: across two verses of a poem of John Oxenham(s in
an old magazine and it seened so very true of China at the presert that I
pass it on to you to share.
The Dawn.
"I see a new world rising from the ashes of the past,
For when the soul of life blazed out at Froudomss trumpet blnst,
The old false gods of former days and the things that made for shame
Are shriveled in the incense of that sacrificial flame.
From these dread times of suffering,from the shadowy vale cf death,
A sweeter life is springing which is quickened by our faith.
We are building God's new toaples with our blood, our prayers, our teass,
Temples of grace that rise apace and shall outlast the years."
Goodbye. ..... The joy of the Lord keer you ever young in Hin.
Glndys Stephenson.
Kolhapur,India, August 24,1938.
Dear Friends,
Miss Stephenson wrote the above letter to me as a personal friond and

Fonal protest in Washington,D.C., and I an sending copies of this lettor to
all my friends asking them to protest nnd to socure the co-operation of all

ing until our country puts a stop to its share in this terrible torture of
the Chinese poople. We do not need for England to take the lead. We should
not. It is our own responsibility.
will you sond in your protest ? And will you get all other Christians
possible to do the same ? Lot us not ease until our ppotest is heard and
answered.

Beoretary to the President
Sir:
It is possibly sending coels to Newcastle to add enything
more to your burden in regard to internationel relationships.
However, as chairmen of the Committee Against International
Aggression, I wish to express to you the sentiment of the people
in this part of the country as well as my own personal opinion.
We feel thet continued shipments of munitions of wer,
machinery for their manufecture and the materiels from which they
894.24/404
are made is a disgrace to the merchants of our country, as well
as to the nation as a whole.
We, by treaty and by sacred pledge, would pretend to be
friends of China end against the aggressor, Japan. Yet we con-
tinually injure our friendly nation and aid the aggressor nation
and one might easily say without possibility of doubt, merica's
potential if not active enemy; the most destructive agent of
world peace today.
Your citizens believe that if you wish to stop this traffic
a letter from you to these merchants of death, or a radio mes-
sage to your people, would stop the traffic overnight.
Some weeks ago a letter was sent to you suggesting that now
the United States government should notify Jepan that no money
would be loaned to her for the development of the territories
she is stealing from her neighbor, end that our nation would not
FIET
in future recognize any territories so acquired. Thousands of
your people are convinced that this action should be taken "now'
In these days of international perfidy es exhibited by the
British and Frenoh treatment of Czechoslovakia, honest people
the world over stand aghast. It seems that the solemn obliga-
A
tions of the statesmen of the world and the nations they repre-
sent is of far less value than a scrap of paper. It is so pu-
trid that the stenoh rises to high heaven.

The Prime Minister of Great Britain sits down with the colos-
sal bully and bluffer of the world and determines to plunge a
knife into the heart of Czechoslovakia. Then the same Prime
Minister, the Premier of France and the American Ambassador sit
around the conference table and determine to dismember a
friendly state whose representative is excluded from the hearing.
We doubt if history can disclose a more wanton act of inter-
national brigandage.
e do not esk that our people should be brought into the
unholy mess in curope - but in the name of the Lord God Jeho-
vah, we do esk that our President and our Secrotary of State
and our representetives in Europe refuse to be parties to the
paremount treachery of the ages.
The millions of your citizens don't know what to do, but
we do beg of our representatives that they "deal justly, love
mercy" in their netionel end internetionel affeirs lest our
nation be destroyed as were the nations of old to whom the
prophets spoke.

RBSOLUTION ON CHINA

in orodits to Japan whioh help continue the war,

the present conflict,

at the Y.V.C.A. in Now Haven Conneoticut on Soptomber 28th
urges the United States to place a complete embargo on Japan,
inoluding arms, ammunition, gasoline and raw materials until
such time as Japan withdraws from China, and

President Franklin D. Roosevelt and Seoretary of Stete Cordell

ROCKWELL KENT
AUSABLE FORKS
NEW YORK
October 10, 1938.
President Franklin D. Roosevelt,
The White House,
Washington, D. C.
My dear Mr. President:
Citizens of a democratic country must never forget that
their government must be practically representative of
their interests, nor that 1t 1s their right at any time
to address such complaints to their President as they may
feel the public interest, or public opinion, warrant.
You, Mr. President, were returned to your second term in
office by an overwhelming majority of the American people.
Their vote was testimony of their confidence that you and
your Administration would truly represent their int erests.
While in most affairs the conduct of the Administration
undoubtedly still has the enthusiastio support of the
majority of the American people, there is one important
matter of foreign policy in which the conduct of the Admin-
1stration must be said to be completely misrepresentative
of the will of even the majority of the Administration's
own supporters. It 1s in regard to the shipment of arms
and supplies to Japan.
The Administration is of course fully aware of the grow-
ing indignation of the American public against our contin-
ued alding and abetting of aggressors in wars of conquest.
Please accept this letter as a citizen's properly indig-
nant protest at our official violation of democratio and
1nternation morality, and a warning from one who has been
a staunch supporter of the Administration, that unless
our foreign policy 1s made to be representative of the
wishes of the American people, the Administration will
forfeit the People's support.
I have the honor to be, my dear sir,
Your obedient servant,
ROCKWELL KENT.
RK:fk

In connection with the reference in
the attached letter to protection of mis-
sionary property in China a reply 1s sug-
gested along the following lines:
"During the course of the
present conflict in the Far East
the American Government has em-
ployed all practicable means to
protect American lives and has
protested against and sought to
obtain satisfaction for damages
to and interference with Ameri-
can missionary and commercial
interests. It has shown that it
demands respect for the rights
and safety of our people and at
the same time it has avoided in-
volvement in the dieputes of
others. Furthermore, the Ameri-
can Government has informed the
Governments of China and Japan
that it reserves all rights on
1ts own behalf and on behalf of
American citizens in respect of
damages which might result from
military operations. When specific
cases or circumstances arise 1n-
volving Jeopardy to American
nationals, including American
misal onaries,

=== Page 550 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE

-2-
missionaries, or violation of
their rights and interests, the
Amerioan nationals concerned make
report thereof directly to Ameri-
can diplomatic or consular offi-
cers in China who have instructions
as to appropriate procedure to be
followed and who report those cases
and ciroumstances to the Department.
Such cases receive prompt and care-
ful attention and all appropriate
efforts are made to obtain remedial
or redressive action.
"within the last few weeks
American official representatives
in the Far East, especially at
Shanghai, have through representa-
tions to the Japanese authorities
been successful in obtaining the
return to the owners of American
missionary property at Yangchow,
Quinsan, and Soochow, Kiangsu
Province. You may be assured
that both the Department and 1ts
representatives in the field are
continuing their efforts to obtain
full respect for all American
rights and interests in the Far
East."

its Centennial anniversary at : adison, isconsin, witar342
voting delegates present, on sentember 23 unanimotsly
passed the following resolution:
"The Wisconsin Congregational Conference desires to
emphasize Its fraternity and sympathy with the
Christians of China and Japan in the burdens and
strairs imposed by the tragic war between their
nations. Ve would ephasize our friendship with
89

4.24/420

744.24

nations and for those on the verge of war, that a

Dear Secretary Hull:
Run/am
We the teachers, officers, and pupils of the Chinese Sunday
School of Central Methodist Church, Detroit, have for many months been
deeply aware of the suffering on the part of both Chinese and Japanese,
resulting from the war in China. We have sent considerably over a
thousand dollars to China for relief work, but now we feel that we
should do something fundamental toward stopping this fratricide. In
this we ask your help.
.894.24/422
We are loyal American citizens and are proud of our country's
long record of friendship for China. We believe that most Americens
are unalterably opposed to Japan's policy of military aggression in
China, and would look upon a triumphant Japan as a menace to America's
best interests in the Orient. Yet we are amazed at the extent to which
Japan is being aided in the conflict with China by American-made
ammmition and supplies. We do not think that the will of the American
people should be thwarted and the best interests of our country
sacrificed in order that a relatively few companies should make huge
profits from this unworthy traffic with the Japanese militarists. As an
aggressor nation, as a treaty-breaker, and as a perpetrator of inhuman
atrocities, Japan should be quarantined by the nations which believe in
peace and democracy. It would be a kindness to both the Japanese people
and the Chinese if Japan's supply of war materials could be cut off.
Does it not smack of hypocracy for the American government to protest
against the bombing of civilians in Canton or Hankow and at the same
time permit American citizens to continue to provide the weapons and
the fuel by which the bombings are made possible?
We realize of course that you must represent the vested
interests of the industrialists as well as pro-Chinese groups like our
own, and that they are more vocal than we. A strict embargo on war
materials would necessarily deprive them of profits, but their sacrifice
will be small indeed compared with that which the world will pay if
Japan ever establishes itself effectively in China.
W
B
Sincerely yours,
Johw S. Fatt
Superintendent
Os. stamill

=== Page 581 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to Despatch No.1666
dated SEP 19 1938 from the Amer1-
can Embassy, Moscow, U.S.S.R., on
the subject of a translation en-
titled "Wall Street and Japanese
Aggression in China".
SOURCE: Izvestiya, Moscow, September 10,
1938.
(Organ of the Soviets of Deputies
of the Toilers of the U.S.S.R.)
TRANSLATION.
WALL STREET AND JAPANESE AGGRESSION IN CHINA.
By K. Volkov.
From the very beginning of Japanese aggression in
China it became palpably apparent that a certain part
of the manufacturers and financiers of the U.S.A. in-
directly and directly supported the bandit war of Japan
against the Chinese people. This support is expressed
by supplying Japan with important types of strategic
raw materials and armaments, without which the latter
would not be in a position to conduct a war of any length.
V.I. Lenin, even in 1920 in his speech at the 2nd
Congress of the Comintern pointed out that:
". . . Japan has been able to loot eastern
Asiatic countries, but it cannot have any inde-
pendent financial and military force without the
support of some other country." . . . (Lenin,
Collected Works, v. 25, p. 340.)
This exceptionally clear and exhaustive Lenin
charaoterization of Japan maintains its validity at the
present time. Now even the bourgeois American press
is compelled to recognize that the course of military
activities in China is determined to a considerable de-
ree by the attitude of the London and New York stook
markets

=== Page 582 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
markets toward the present Japanese aggression against
the Chinese people. Thus, for example, the New York
Times on July 8, 1938, writes that along with China
"London and New York are fields of battle, where the
outcome of the present war on the Asiatic continent is
being decided".
An attentive study of the facts shows, however, tht
the London and New York stock markets in this connection
are not at all arenas of bitter fighting. Wall Street,
the citadel of world capitalism, presents rather an
idyllic picture of a peaceful deal of American magnates
of financial capital with Japanese imperialism. An ar-
ticle published in the journal Oriental Economist by
George Bogen*, editor-in-chief of the magazine New York
Journal of Commerce, serves as a clear illustration of
this deal. This article, entitled "American Capital in
Northern China", besides its contents presents great
interest also because its author expresses not so much
the personal viewpoint of a private person as the views
of the so-called "business" circles in the U.S.A. George
Bogen begins with the frank statement:
"Industrial and financial ciroles in the
United States in general do not share the wide-
spread indignation against the Japanese occupation
of Northern China and Shanghai. They are more in-
olined to hold the realistio point of view that
Japan requires room for economic expansion and
that China is precisely such a field of activity
for Japanese expansion. Such a point of view, in
partioular, is the reason that the movement for a
boyoott of Japanese goods has not attained serious
dimensions. In reality industrial and financial
oircles
Transliteration from the Russian.

=== Page 583 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
circles of the U.S.A. have made special efforts
to weaken this boycott movement in every way."
This statement does not recuire comment. George
Bogen and "business" circles of the U.S.A., it seems,
not only openly approve and encourage the rapacious
policy of Japanese imperialism in China, but directly
admit that they are disrupting the anti-Japanese boycott
in the U.S.A. precisely with the aim of rendering aid to
the aggressor.
George Bo en further waxes even more eloquent. He
points out that "financial circles of the U.S.A. are
harboring serious fears with regard to the condition af
Japanese finances" in connection with the war in China;
and he expresses the supposition that the present economic
measures of the Japanese Government for curtailing the im-
portation of a number of types of goods from the U.S.A.
may harm the interests of American exporters. Neverthe-
less Bogen continues:
"The present enormous purchases by Japan of
various goods and in particular of oil has produced
a favorable impression on the branches of industry
of the U.S.A. directly interested. These branches
of industry have shown a great readiness to view
the existing situation from the point of view of
Japanese interests. However, universal indignation
with regard to the activities of Japan has been so
strong in the U.S.A. that up to the present time
our finanoiers and industrialists have avoided makig
any public declaration with regard to their position
on the question, although they certainly were ex-
tremely interested in the fact that the Government
of the U.S.A. has proorastinated about applying the
noutrality law."
Thus, the bankers and the oil, steel and cotton
kings in the U.s.Á. do not now even consider it necessary
to

=== Page 584 === (confidence 0.99)
-4 -
to conceal the fact that behind the back of public opinion
of the U.S.A. and in spite of it they have blessed the ra-
pacious policy of Japan in China and have rendered it as-
sistance in every way. It is fully evident that the posi-
tion of the financial oligarchy of the U.S.A. on this
question does not correspond to the policy of the present
Government of the U.S.A. which, as before, although not
consistently enough, is in f vor of the maintenance of the
territorial integrity of China and against war and ag-
gress on as an instr ment of national policy. However,
this does not in the least disturb the wirepullers of
Wall Street. If they have often opposed the measures of
Roosevelt's Government inside the country and have fre-
quently disrupted them, there is nothing astonishing in
the fact that these circles have actually repudiated and
also have frequently and simply sabotaged the measures
of foreign policy of the Roosevelt Administration.
George Bogen assures Japanese bankers and manu-
facturers that "business" circles of the U.S.A. would
have no objection to participating in the exploitation
of the riches of Northern China under the aegis of the
Japanese usurpers. However, the fate of the Ámerican oil
companies in Manchuria is still too well remembered in
the U.S.A. and therefore George Bogen considers it not
superfluous to demand firm guarantees that future American
investments in China be exempted from the restrictions
and arbitrary actions of the Japanese authorities,
partioularly

=== Page 585 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
particularly the military. The author, taking for
granted the problematical Japanese "victory", ends his
article with the following advice to his Japanese col-
leagues:
"Apparently, the surest method for removing
the hesitation among American busines circles
on the question of participution in the financing
of North China would be the publication of a joint
statement by the Japanese Government, the Army and
the Autonomous Government of Northern China, which
would express their friendly attitude toward
foreign investments, and in which they would under-
take to avoid repression and limitation with regard
to foreign trade and investments on the territory
of Northern China."
The article of George Bogen is not at all the idle
thoughts of an American journalist; it is quite suffi-
ciently reinforced by facts. The course of events shows
that word and deed are in accord among the business men
of Wall Street. A series of data confirms the presence
of systematic aid, rendered by American capitalists to
the Japanese aggressor. It is extremely indicative that
American financiers and manufacturers, who have invested
about 400 million dollars in various Japanese enter-
prises (machine construction, chemicals, etc.), have not
made the slightest attempt to protest against the estab-
lishment of governmental control over those branohes of
production in which American capital is particularly in-
terested. It is well known that the representative of
the Ford Company in Japan has subsoribed heavily to a
loan issued by the Japanese Government to finance the
war in China. In the foreign press there has not yet
been any authentic information regarding the granting
of

=== Page 586 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
of a large loan to Japan by American financiers. However,
the magazine Far Eastern Survey, well informed on these
questions, hinted unambiguously enough as far back as
June 15, 1938, that Japanese solicitations along this line
had received a certain encouragement and even a promise
of the grant of a loan in the "near future" on the part
of individual banks in the U.S.A. The picture becomes
even more indicative if we turn to the question of the
enormous extent to which Japan is bein€ supplied with
strategic raw materials and armaments from the U.S.A.
It is scarcely necessary to speak of the enormous
role of oil in modern warfare. The Japanese Government,
taking into account the fact that the internal sourcas of
oil hardly cover 10; to 12 of the total requirements of
the country, began in a dvance to create large stores of
this most important strategic raw material. American
economists estimate that on the eve of the invasion a
China the Naval Ministry of Japan had at its disposal stores
of 20 million barrels of oil, which nearly equals the annual
requirement for the fleet and army in time of peace. How-
ever, even by the third month of war Japan has been com-
pelled to increase considerably the purchase of dl in
the U.S.A. According to information published in the
March issue of the magazine Pacific Affairs by Eliot
Janeway, economic expert of the International Statistical
Bureau, Japan has purchased 25 million gallons of aviation
casoline in California during the first six days af Sep-
tember l937 alone. In the course of the last two weeks
of

=== Page 587 === (confidence 0.99)
-7 -
of the same month there were purchased in the U.S.A.
105 million gallons of oil, suitable for refining into
aviation gasoline. In all, during the months of Sep-
tember and October, Japan purchased 337 million gallons
of oil in the U.S.A! In other words Japan bought nearly
twice as much oil daily as in the year of 1936 (135,000
barrels daily in 1937 in comparison with 72,000 barrels
in 1936). According to official data of American sta-
tistics, published in the magazine Far Eastern Survey,
during the whole year of 1937 Japan imported 35 million
barrels of oil from the U.S.A., i.e., nearly twice as
much as the annual store in peace time. This is not
counting purchases in the Dutch East Indies and ot her
countries.
It is possible to observe a similar picture also
in the field of export of American steel and iron to
Jepan. Japan, preparing for war against the Chinese
people, at the beginning of 1937 began enormous pur-
chases of iron ore and scrap iron abroad. E. Janeway,
mentioned by us before, asserts that during the first
four months of 1937 the United States sold Japan more
pig iron in bars than was sold to any other country during
the previous six years! In 1937 the U.S.A. sold to Japan
990,000 tons of iron and steel; during the same period
in 1936 only 40,000 tons in al1 were sold! During 1937
Japan imported from abroad in all about 1,900,000 tons
of sorap iron, which is double the import of l936.
Japan not only imports sorap iron in large amounts
from

=== Page 588 === (confidence 0.99)
- 8 -
from the U.S.A., but also high-grade steels (chromium
nickel, aluminum, etc.). In 19. 8 the amount of steel
and iron ore imported by Japan from the U.S.A. not only
did not diminish, but actually increased. The magazine
Far Eastern Survey, already cited by us, of June 1, 1938,
reports that during the first quarter of 1938 Japan im-
prted 172,816 tons of iron and steel from the U.S.A.
against 87,907 tons for the first quarter of 1937, i.e.,
an increase of nearly 100%! The sume E. Janeway in his
article in the June issue of the magazine Asia stated
that in the field of machine construction Japan is almost
entirely dependent on Detroit and Pittsburgh for the
special steels of airplane construction and automobile
industries.
No less indicative is the Japanese importation of
modern tools and machines. One may say without exag-
geration that the Japanese machine-building industry,
including military machine-building as well, is now
being reorganized on the basis of American technical
assistance and according to American methods. By the
calculations of E. Janeway, in 1937 Japan purchased
machines to a value of $3,000,000 monthly in the U.S.A.
By value imports by Japan of machine equipment from the
U.S.A. equal 50% of the cost of all equipment produced
within the country in l937. Moreover, according to the
opinion of American experts, this figure is far from
reflecting the actual state of affairs, since an impor-
tant part of Japanese orders for the most intricate
•quipment has not yet been filled by American factories
and

=== Page 589 === (confidence 0.99)
- 9 -
and the final amount of the export of tools to Japan
can be given only in the second half of 1938. According
to the data presented in the July issue of the magazine
Far Eastern Front, Japan in 1937 imported machines and
tools to a value of 53 million dollars, of which 33 million
dollars fell to the share of the U.S.A.
At present Japan is suffering serious difficulties
in railway transport. A large cuantity of freight is
not transported as a result of the shortage of locomo-
tives and rolling stock. The plan for the construction
of 500 new locomotives and 7,000 cars has not been ful-
filled in view of the fact that the 24 factories which
received orders for their construction do not possess
the required number of turning lathes and other machines.
And here again the American capitalists came to the aid
of Japan. Railroad and steel kings in the U.S.A. under-
took to deliver the necessary tools to the Jpanese fac-
tories.
This is also approximately the picture in the auto-
mobile industry. Not long after the beginning of the war
in China the Japanese tried to force foreign capital out
of this branch of industry ("General Motors" and Ford).
However, the war soon showed that the quality of Japanese
automobile stands at an ertremely low level. In the
Chinese theater of military activities Japanese automo-
biles quickly broke down and got out of order. They
were foroed to make peace with both Ford and "General
Motors", whioh are now the chief suppliers of automobile
transportation

=== Page 590 === (confidence 0.99)
- 10 -
transportation in Japan nd the organizers of its au-
tomobile industries.
The aviation industry goes without saying. Japan
is now trying to start on the road of mass production
of airplanes, mainly according to American models; but
until such time as this production is mastered it is
purchasing finished products made in the U.S.A. In
1937 and 1938 Japan was the largest purchaser of Am-
erican airplanes. Thus, for example, the magazine
Great Britain and the East reports that during May 1938
alone, Japan bought airplanes in the U.S.A. valued at
more than $500,000. We note in passing that the 1arge
purchases of aviation gasoline in the U.S.A. are ex-
plained by the fact that the majority of Japanese air-
planes and motors either are built according to American
models or are directly imported from the U.S.A.
A general idea of the extent of the export of war
materials and arms from the U.S.A. to Japan is given in
the official figures of the Department of Commerce of
the U.S.A., which were published in the New York Time s
on August 12, 1938. During the first half of 1938 ex-
ports from the U.S.A. to Japan amounted by value to
$124,958,000, which in comparison with the figures for
the first half of 1937 ($165,611,000) shows a decrease
of $40,653,000. This decline does not at all indicate
a decrease in the military purchases of Japan in the
U.s.Á. The figures previously presented by us tell pre-
eisely the contrary story. The decrease of imports
from

=== Page 591 === (confidence 0.99)
- 11 -
from the U.S.A. occurred because of the sharp decline
in the import of consumers goods and of raw materials
not intended for the needs of war.
To the scale of American military supplies to Japan
the amount of gold exported from Japan to the U.S.A.
eloquently bears witness. According to the data of the
New York Federal Reserve Bank, from March 1937 to January
1938, 329 million gold dollars, or more than one-third of
the entire gold reserve of Japan, had already been trans-
ferred to the safes of American bankers.
All of these facts testify that American magnates
have actually placed the unlimited economic resources
of the U.S.A. at the disposal of the Japanese aggressor.
However, the deal of the financial oligarchy of the
U.S.A. with Japanese imperialism not only frees the hands
of the Japanese aggressor in its destructive war against
the Chinese people, but also basicly undermines the fun-
damentals of the traditional policy of the U.S.A. in the
Far East.
Durig the course of the last decades the most far-
sighted American political figures have emphasized more
than onoe that the Government of the U.S.A. is obliged to
take very effeotive measures to put a stop to any aid on
the part of the U.S.A. to aggressor countries which vio-
late the territorial integrity of China.
Thomas Millard, a very great expert on the Far East,
expounded this requirement with distinot clarity in his
memorandum

=== Page 592 === (confidence 0.98)
- 12 -
memorandum addressed to the Senate back in 1919. He
wrote that the cornerstone of the policy of the U.S.A.
in the Far East is:
"The refusal on the part of the U.S.A. to render
any aid to those countries which can use it di-
rectly or indirectly to the detriment of American
interests in Eastern Asia; the immediate cutting
off of financial or any other support to those
countries which directly or indirectly try to
violate the territorial integrity of China and
the policy of the 'open door' in this country."
However, reality has shown that demands of this
type by the most far-sighted Americans are still far
from transfomation into reality. It is true that in
their recent speeches the most prominent political fi-
gures of the U.S.A. call upon public opinion of their
country to exert moral pressure upon the capitalists
who are supplying Japan with arms and strategic ma-
terials. The very near future will show how effective
such a pious method of reforming the inveterate sinners
of Wall Street will be.

Dear Madam:
I received this morning your letter of October 20th,
with enclosures, discussing the resolutions passed by the
New Haven meeting and sent to you.
Apparently the State Department misuncerstood the
purpose of the resolution on the Far East and I have
written trying to explain what I thought was in the minds
of those who voted for the resolution.
I enclose herewith a copy of the letter, which I
request that you bring to Senator Maloney's attention,
since, as you know, the resolution 1s addressed to
Congress as well as to the Presiaent.
Respectfully yours,
CHASE KIMBALL

=== Page 610 === (confidence 0.99)
THE FIRST CONGREGATIONAL CHURCH
Dalton, Massachusetts
Harry W. Foot
October 19, 1938.
Minister
Honorable Allen T. Treadway,
Congressional Office Building,
Washington, D. C.
Dear Sir:
As scribe of the Berkshire North Association of
Congregational Christian churches it is my duty to trans-
mit to you an expresslon of opinion on the matter of
Sino-Japanese foreign policy growing out of the Annual
Meeting of the Association held in South Williamstown,
Massachusetts, on October 18, 1938. The Association com-
prises twenty-one churches and twenty-six ministers of
the First Congressional istrict.
The Association voted to go on record as being
heartily opposed to the further exportation of munitions
of war to Japan. Its members are persuaded that the con-
flict represents a caee of aggression directly contrary to
every Christian ideal of international procedure.
The Association believes that we as a nation are
responsible for the continuance of the war in-as-much as
we are furnishing the aggressor natlon with eighty-five
percent of the warstuffs which make the invasion of China
possible.
The Association therefore respectfully asks that you
use your influence to curtail and terminnte the exporta-
tion of war materials to Japan.
We feel that only by so doing can we as Americans
redeem our birthright and help make possible for less-
privileged nations the destiny of freedom which we cherish
as our own.
Respectfully yours,
HARRY W. FOOT, scribe
Berkshire North Association.

somewhat along the following lines:
It will be observed that Seation 1 (a) of this
Joint Resolution provides that, whenever the Presi-
dent shall find that there exists a state of war be-

proelaim suek fast. Should the President issme a

Neutrality Act. In the absenee of such a proelame-
tion, however, there exists no legal authority for
preventing the export of arms either to both those
oountries or to Japan alone.
Moreover, the Neutrality Act does not under any
oiroumstances authorize the President or the Seere-
tary of State to prohibit the export of oommodities
other than arms, ammumition and implements of war.
The articles and materials whioh are considered to
be arms, ammunitions, and implements of war are
emmerated in the President's Proolamation of May 1,
1937, whioh 1s set forth under Part II of the pamphlet.
It may be of interest to know that, sinoe July 1,
1937, the Department of State has 1ssued 1oenses for
the export of arms, ammmition, and implements of war
to China in the value of $14, 265, 554.11 and to Japan
1n the value of $10,793,865.38.
I may add, for your information, that the primary pur-
pose of the Joint Resolution of May 1, 1937, is to keep
this country out of war. The question as to whether and
when the provisions of the Joint Resolution shall be invoked
1s properly left to the sound discretion of the President.
In deciding whether he should invoke the Joint Resolution
in a partioular situation, the President must take into ao-
count various faotors and partioularly the broad, general
interests of this country and its nationals. In the present
situstion in the Far East, the President has coneluded that,
in the light of all the facts and siroumstanees, when weighed
frem a praetieal point of view, it would not be to the best
interests of this country and our people as a whole to in-
veke the previsioms of this Joint Resolution.

My dear Mr. Michener:
I acknowledge the receipt of your letter of Oetober 12,
19s8, in regard to the export of war supplies to Japan and
Germany.
894.24/432
As you doubtless are aware, the Neutrality Aot of
May 1, 1937, a copy of whioh is enolosed for your conven1-
ent reference, provides that the President shall, whenever
he finds that a state of war exists between or among two
or more foreign countries, proolaim such fact and that there-
after the export of arms, ammunition, and implements of war
to both parties to the conflict shall be prohibited. There
is no statute now in existence which would permit the ap-
plieation of such an embargo to the export of arms to one
party to the confliot and not to the other. At the presemt
time, there has been no finding by the President that a
state of war exists with respeet to either Japen or Germamy.
wish
F/A
Mr. Trank W. Miehemer,
Nowberg, Oregen.

=== Page 630 === (confidence 0.96)

With regard to the applioation of the Neutrality Aot
to the situation in the Far East, I may point out that the
primary purpose of this Act is to keep this country out of
war. The question as to whether and when the provisions
of the Joint Resolution shall be invoked is properly left
to the sound disoretion of the President. In deoiding
whether he should invoke the Joint Resolution in a partioular
situation the President must take into acoount various fao-
tors and partioularly the broed, general interests of this
country and its nationals. In the present situation in the
Far Kast, the Prèsident has vonoluded that, in the light
of all the facts and ciroumstanoes, when weighed from a
practioal point of view, it would not be to the best in-
terests of this country and our people as a whole to in-
voke the provisions of this Joint Resolution.
The Neutrality Aot, moreover, does not authorise the
President or the Secretary of State under any ciroumstances
to proktbit the export of commodities other than arms, am-
munition, and implements of war. The artioles and materials
mhiek are coasidered to be arms, ammunition, and implements

that, whenever the President shall find that there exists
e state of war between or among two or more foreign states,

a preelemation finding that a state of war exists between
Chima and Japan, the expart of arms, ammunition, and imple-

Joint Resolution shall be invoked is properly left to the
sound discretion of the President. In deciding whether

ASSOCIATION OF THE METROPOLITAN AREA OF NEW YORK

New York, New York
COMMUNICATIONS
October 17, 1938
AND RECORDS
The Honorable Cordell Hull
Secretary of State
Washington, D. C.
894.24/
Dear Secretary Hull:
WHEREAS, the people of the United States desire peace and deplore international
lawlessness and aggression, and
WHEREAS, the Japanese invasion of China, fomented by the military party of
Japan, has led the United States of America and many other nations to indict
Japan as a violator of the Nine-Power Treaty and the Kellogg Pact, and to pro- 4
test repeatedly against the merciless bombing of undefended Chinese cities, the 
unnecessary slaughter of Chinese civilians, and the destruction and imperilment 6
of schools, churches, hospitals, and business enterprises in which their nationals
are engaged, and
WHEREAS, we feel put to shame that American raw materials are being used to manu-
facture weapons of war in Japan, that American planes are being used to carry
bombs from America for the destruction of Chinese civilian populations, that
shells from America are loading Japanese guns, and that America is supplying
to Japan huge quantities of scrap iron, oil, trucks, and other materials used
in the furtherance of the war, over and above the amount of our normal trade of
recent years, and
WHEREAs, we sympathize with the Chinese people in their desire to build up an
independent, democratic state, an end gravely threatened by the present aggression
of Japan, and deplore the traffic which makes it possible for certain American
interests to profit from the wanton destruction of the innocent victins of
Japanese aggression,
THEREFORE, we the undersigned respectfully petition the Secretary of State, in
accordance with the prinoiple of the Good Neighbor and in the interests of
the people of the United States, China and Japan, to take every practicable
means, diroot or indirect, to bring to an end a traffic from our country which
is compelling us to be a partner in the destruction of the Chinese people.
AND we desire to go on record as expressing our moral support for labot,
in the sale or shipment ofvar supplies to Japan.

Signed WALTER A. R. MCPHERSON

The Honorable Franklin D. Roosevelt
President of the United States
Washington, D. C.
Dear President Roosevelt:
WHEREAS, the people of the United States desire peace and deplore international
lawlessness and aggression, and
WHEREAS, the Japanese invasion of China, fononted by the military party of Japan,
has led the United States of Anerica and many other nations to indict Japan as
a violator of the Nine-Power Treaty and the Kellcgg Pact, and to protest re-
peatedly against the merciless bombing of undefended Chinese cities, the unneces-
sary slaughter of Chinese civilians, and the destruction and imperilment of schools,
churches, hospitals, and business enterprises in which their nationals are engaged,
and
WHEREAS, we feel put to shame that American raw materials are being used to manu-
facture weapons of war in Japan, that American planes are being used to carry
bombs from America for the destruction of Chinese civilian populations, that shells
from America are loading Japanese guns, and that America is supplying to Japan huge
quantities of scrap iron, oil, trucks, and other materiels used in the furtherance
of the war, over and above the amount of our normal trade of recent years, and
WHEREAS, we sympathize with the Chinese people in their desire to build up an
independer, denocratic state, an end gravely threatened by the present aggression
of Japan, and deplore the t raffic which makes it possible for certain American
interests to profit from thevanton destruction of the innocent victims of Japanese
aggression,
THEREFORE, we the undersigned respectfully petition the President of the United
States and Congress, in accordance with the principle of the Good Neighbor and
in the interests of the people of the United States, China and Japan, to take
every practicable means, direct or indirect, to bring to an end a traffic from
our country which is compelling us to be a partner in the destruction of the
Chinese people.
AND we desire to go on record as expressing our moral support for labor, financial,
industrial, commercial, and other groups whichrefuse to participate in the sale
or shipment of war supplies to Japan.
Signed WALTER A. R. MCPHERSON
WAYNE A. NICHOLAS

Dear Secretary Hull:-
894.24
Through other organizations I have voiced
894.24/443
my opposition to the sale of war materials to the Japanese Gov-
ernment, which war materials an used to defeat and exterminate
the Chinese people. What deeply grieves me is that we sent mis-
sionaries to China and Japan to convert these peoples to the
Christian religion, and the Christian religion stands against
war, and then our Government permits the sale of war materials
to the Japanese nation so that the Japanese can carry on a most
ruthless war against an innocent people.
In yesterday's local paper there is shown
a picture of a Japanese ship loading lead, purchased from the
citizens of the United States that it may be used to mold bull-
ets with which to exterminate the Chinese. We as individuals
cannot do anything to stop this procedure, but it is within the
power of our Government to do so. I would herewith again voice
my protest against this wicked and inhuman procedure of our
people being permitted to make money out of this wicked war again-
st the Chinese people. God cannot look with favor on a Government
that will permit that. Surely we as a people are not so poverty
